<a id="pm-s1"></a>
## 1. Abstract and result contract

> We prove the Fourier-vanishing conjecture of Matolcsi, Ruzsa, and Weiner: every complex Hadamard matrix of order six outside Tao's cubic equivalence class has a vanishing character sum at every permutation of $`(1,1,1,-1,-1,-1)`$. We also give an exact certificate excluding seven mutually unbiased bases in ℂ<sup>6</sup>; by Weiner's completion theorem, this yields an upper bound of five. Both certificates use only integer and rational arithmetic, and the complete verifier is included.

- **claim-fourier-vanishing** (theorem, [source](#stmt-thm-fourier)): Let $H$ be a complex Hadamard matrix of order six. If $H$ is not equivalent to the cubic matrix $T$ in <a href="#eq:cubic-matrix" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="eq:cubic-matrix">[eq:cubic-matrix]</a>, then $$g_H(\pi\alpha)=0\qquad\text{for every coordinate permutation }\pi.$$
  - Explanation: Every order-six complex Hadamard matrix not equivalent to the cubic matrix has vanishing character sums at all coordinate permutations of the balanced charge alpha, proving the Fourier-vanishing conjecture.
- **claim-no-complete-family** (theorem, [source](#stmt-thm-main)): There do not exist seven orthonormal bases $B_r=(b_{r,0},\ldots,b_{r,5})$ of $\mathbb C^6$, $1\le r\le7$, such that $$\bigl\|\langle b_{r,i},b_{s,j}\rangle\bigr\|^2=\frac16 \qquad(r\ne s,\ 0\le i,j\le5).$$
  - Explanation: No seven orthonormal bases of the six-dimensional complex space can be pairwise mutually unbiased, so no complete family exists in this dimension.
- **claim-dichotomy** (theorem, [source](#stmt-cor-dichotomy)): For every $6\times6$ Hadamard matrix $H$, at least one of the following conditions holds:* 1. *$g_{H^\pi}(\alpha)=0$ for every row permutation $\pi$;* 2. *the entrywise square $H^{\circ2}$ is a Hadamard matrix.
  - Explanation: For any order-six Hadamard matrix, either all permuted-alpha characters vanish for every row ordering, or the entrywise square is again Hadamard.
- **lemma-order-one-contraction** (theorem, [source](#stmt-lem-contraction)): Let $A$ be a balanced exponent matrix with $p$ rows and $q\leq6$ columns, and suppose row $i$ has order one. For $\ell\ne i$, let $A_{i\to\ell}$ be obtained by adding row $i$ to row $\ell$ and then deleting row $i$. If $p\leq6$, then $$(7-p)m_H(A) =-\sum_{\substack{0\leq\ell<p\\\ell\ne i}} m_H(A_{i\to\ell}). \label{eq:injective-contraction}$$ If $p=7$, the valid relation is instead $$\sum_{\substack{0\leq\ell<7\\\ell\ne i}} m_H(A_{i\to\ell})=0. \label{eq:seven-row-contraction}$$ Every moment in <a href="#eq:seven-row-contraction" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="eq:seven-row-contraction">[eq:seven-row-contraction]</a> has at most six rows; no moment with seven injectively sampled rows is defined or used. The analogous relations hold for columns by transposition.
  - Explanation: A row of order one in an exponent matrix can be contracted into the other rows, giving a linear relation among injective moments; the seven-row case yields a homogeneous relation.
- **lemma-equality-pattern-expansion** (theorem, [source](#stmt-lem-partitions)): Let $a_1,\ldots,a_n\in\mathbb Z^6$ be balanced charges satisfying $\sum_{j=1}^n a_j=0$, and let $A$ be the matrix with these columns. For a set partition $\mathcal S$ of $\{1,\ldots,n\}$, form $A_{\mathcal S}$ by replacing the columns in each block $S\in\mathcal S$ by their sum $\sum_{j\in S}a_j$. Then $$\left\langle \mathop{\mathrm{Re}}\prod_{j=1}^n g_{\widetilde H}(a_j) \right\rangle_H =\sum_{\substack{\mathcal S\text{ a set partition of }\{1,\ldots,n\}\\ \|\mathcal S\|\leq6}} \frac{(6)_{\|\mathcal S\|}}{6^n}\, m_H(A_{\mathcal S}). \label{eq:injective-partitions}$$ The ordering of the blocks of $\mathcal S$ is immaterial.
  - Explanation: The averaged real part of a product of characters expands over set partitions of the factors, with weights counting block assignments, into injective moments of merged-column matrices.
- **prop-single-matrix-certificate** (theorem, [source](#stmt-prop-pair-certificate)): For every $6\times6$ Hadamard matrix $H$ and for each $q\in\{q_{\mathrm{cross}},q_{\mathrm{same}}\}$, $$3\bigl\langle \|L_{\widetilde H}\|^2\bigr\rangle_H +\left\langle \|g_{\widetilde H}(\alpha)g_{\widetilde H}(q)\|^2 \right\rangle_H=0. \label{eq:pair-certificate}$$
  - Explanation: A sum of two nonnegative averaged quantities vanishes identically for both choices of the auxiliary charge, which forces each summand to vanish.
- **prop-cubic-classification** (classification, [source](#stmt-prop-cubic-classification)): Suppose that $H$ and its entrywise square $H^{\circ2}$ are Hadamard matrices of order six. By multiplying rows and columns by unit complex numbers and permuting rows and columns, one can transform $H$ into the matrix $T$ in <a href="#eq:cubic-matrix" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="eq:cubic-matrix">[eq:cubic-matrix]</a>. In particular, every entry of the transformed matrix is a cube root of unity.
  - Explanation: An order-six Hadamard matrix whose entrywise square is also Hadamard is equivalent to the cubic matrix, and so has cube-root-of-unity entries after normalization.
- **lemma-basic-character-identities** (theorem, [source](#stmt-lem-basic)): For each fixed reference basis the following identities hold. First, $$\label{eq:order-two} g_s(d)=0\quad(d\in D), \qquad \sum_{s\ne r}g_s(a)=0\quad(\mathop{\mathrm{ord}}(a)=2).$$ For arbitrary charges $a,b$ and each coordinate $i$, $$\label{eq:same-matrix} \sum_{j=0}^5 g_s(a+e_i-e_j)g_s(b-e_i+e_j)=g_s(a+b).$$ The same identity holds with both shifts reversed. For distinct external bases $s,t\ne r$, $$\label{eq:different-matrices} \sum_{d\in D}g_s(a+d)g_t(b-d)=0.$$
  - Explanation: For a family of mutually unbiased bases and a fixed reference basis, characters vanish at order-one differences, sum to zero at order two over the external bases, and satisfy shift identities within and across transition matrices.
- **prop-cubic-exclusion** (theorem, [source](#stmt-prop-cubic-exclusion)): In a set of seven mutually unbiased bases in $\mathbb C^6$, no transition Hadamard matrix $H$ can have $H^{\circ2}$ Hadamard.
  - Explanation: Inside a seven-basis unbiased set no transition matrix can have a Hadamard entrywise square, excluding the cubic alternative by an exact orbit-average contradiction.
- **lemma-cubic-symmetry** (theorem, [source](#stmt-lem-cubic-symmetry)): Every permutation of the rows of $T$ can be completed to a symmetry of $T$ by column permutations, row and column phase factors, and, if necessary, complex conjugation. Consequently $\|G(b)\|$ is constant on each orbit of balanced charges under permutations and negation.
  - Explanation: Every row permutation of the cubic matrix extends to a symmetry, hence the modulus of its character is constant on orbits under permutation and negation.
- **lemma-gram-entries** (theorem, [source](#stmt-lem-gram)): For balanced $a,v$, put $\mathbf b=(a,-v,v-a)$. If $(s,t)\in\{0,1\}^2$ and $(s,t)\ne(1,1)$, then $$\label{eq:gram-first} \mathop{\mathrm{Re}}\,\mathbb E\bigl(F_s(a)\overline{F_t(v)}\bigr) =\mathcal M((s,t,0);\mathbf b).$$ For the remaining case, $$\label{eq:gram-second} \mathop{\mathrm{Re}}\,\mathbb E\bigl(F_1(a)\overline{F_1(v)}\bigr) =\frac15\mathcal M((1,1,0);\mathbf b) +\frac45\mathcal M((1,2,0);\mathbf b).$$
  - Explanation: Real parts of Gram entries of the shared-column test functions equal three-factor mixed moments, with a weighted combination in the case where both functions are averaged.
- **lemma-mixed-moment-reductions** (theorem, [source](#stmt-lem-mixed-reductions)): Under <a href="#eq:all-M" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="eq:all-M">[eq:all-M]</a>, the following operations and identities are valid.* 1. *A common coordinate permutation of all charges, simultaneous negation of all charges, a simultaneous reordering of factors and their labels, and an arbitrary injective renaming of the used labels preserve <a href="#eq:mixed-moment" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="eq:mixed-moment">[eq:mixed-moment]</a>. A zero charge factor may be omitted. Coordinates on which every charge is zero may also be omitted, with the remaining coordinates interpreted as a uniform injection into the six row positions.* 2. *The moment vanishes if any charge has order one or is a coordinate permutation of $\alpha$.* 3. *Suppose that $a_j$ has order two and its label $s_j$ occurs only once. Let $S$ be the set of distinct used labels and $l=\|S\|$. For each $t\in S\setminus\{s_j\}$, let $\mathbf s^{j\to t}$ be obtained from $\mathbf s$ by replacing $s_j$ with $t$. Then $$\label{eq:singleton-reduction} \mathcal M(\mathbf s;\mathbf a) =-\frac1{7-l}\sum_{t\in S\setminus\{s_j\}} \mathcal M(\mathbf s^{j\to t};\mathbf a).$$* 4. *If all labels are equal, the moment is a linear combination of the pair moments <a href="#eq:average-pair-moment" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="eq:average-pair-moment">[eq:average-pair-moment]</a>. More precisely, let $A$ have columns $a_1,\ldots,a_n$, after removing its zero rows. For a set partition $\mathcal P$ of $\{1,\ldots,n\}$, let $A_{\mathcal P}$ have one column $\sum_{j\in B}a_j$ for each block $B\in\mathcal P$. Then $$\label{eq:single-label-partitions} \mathcal M((s,\ldots,s);\mathbf a) =\sum_{\substack{\mathcal P\text{ a set partition}\\\|\mathcal P\|\le6}} \frac{(6)_{\|\mathcal P\|}}{6^n}\,m(A_{\mathcal P}),$$ where $(6)_k=6\cdot5\cdots(6-k+1)$. Zero rows or columns in $A_{\mathcal P}$ may be deleted as in Section <a href="#sec:pair" data-reference-type="ref" data-reference="sec:pair">3</a>.* 5. *For balanced $a,b,c$ with $a+b+c=0$, $$\begin{aligned} &\sum_{j=0}^5 \mathcal M\bigl((s,s,u); (a+e_i-e_j,b-e_i+e_j,c)\bigr) =\mathcal M\bigl((s,u);(a+b,c)\bigr), \label{eq:mixed-same}\\ &\sum_{d\in D} \mathcal M\bigl((s,t,u);(a+d,b-d,c)\bigr)=0 \qquad(s\ne t). \label{eq:mixed-different} \end{aligned}$$ Equation <a href="#eq:mixed-same" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="eq:mixed-same">[eq:mixed-same]</a> also holds with the signs of both shifts reversed. The third label $u$ may coincide with another label.
  - Explanation: Under the vanishing assumption, mixed moments obey symmetry, vanishing, singleton-label reduction, single-label partition expansion and shift-sum identities.
- **prop-mixed-certificate** (bound, [source](#stmt-prop-mixed-certificate)): Each matrix $Z_\lambda$ specified above is positive definite. Under the mixed-moment identities of Lemma <a href="#lem:mixed-reductions" data-reference-type="ref" data-reference="lem:mixed-reductions">12</a>, the forms <a href="#eq:Young-functions" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="eq:Young-functions">[eq:Young-functions]</a> satisfy $$\begin{aligned} 5\sum_\lambda 10^{15-e_\lambda} \mathbb E\bigl(Y_\lambda^*Z_\lambda Y_\lambda\bigr) &\le \frac{-613302797399911+179721388988719}{6480} \label{eq:mixed-certificate}\\ &=-\frac{2007321335237}{30}<0.\notag \end{aligned}$$
  - Explanation: The six specified integer matrices are positive definite and the combined positive form has a strictly negative exact rational upper bound after the moment substitutions.
- **lemma-exact-substitutions** (theorem, [source](#stmt-lem-exact-elimination)): Let $W\in\mathbb Z^{r\times N}$ consist of selected moment equations after their denominators have been cleared. Suppose a set $J$ of $r$ columns gives a square submatrix $W_J$ invertible modulo a prime. Put $F=\{1,\ldots,N\}\setminus J$. If a rational matrix $P\in\mathbb Q^{N\times N}$ satisfies $$P_{F,F}=I,\qquad P_{*,J}=0,\qquad WP=0,$$ then every real vector $\mathbf m$ satisfying $W\mathbf m=0$ obeys $\mathbf m=P\mathbf m$. Thus the rows of $P$ give valid substitutions for all coordinates in terms of the coordinates indexed by $F$.
  - Explanation: A rational matrix satisfying the stated identity and zero-column conditions gives valid substitutions for every real solution of the selected equations, even if moment values are irrational.

      "conclusion": {
        "anchor": "stmt-lem-contraction",
        "exact_quote": "Let $A$ be a balanced exponent matrix with $p$ rows and $q\\leq6$ columns, and suppose row $i$ has order one. For $\\ell\\ne i$, let $A_{i\\to\\ell}$ be obtained by adding row $i$ to row $\\ell$ and then deleting row $i$. If $p\\leq6$, then $$(7-p)m_H(A)\n    =-\\sum_{\\substack{0\\leq\\ell<p\\\\\\ell\\ne i}}\n        m_H(A_{i\\to\\ell}).\n  \\label{eq:injective-contraction}$$ If $p=7$, the valid relation is instead $$\\sum_{\\substack{0\\leq\\ell<7\\\\\\ell\\ne i}}\n      m_H(A_{i\\to\\ell})=0.\n  \\label{eq:seven-row-contraction}$$ Every moment in <a href=\"#eq:seven-row-contraction\" data-reference-type=\"eqref\" data-reference=\"eq:seven-row-contraction\">[eq:seven-row-contraction]</a> has at most six rows; no moment with seven injectively sampled rows is defined or used.         "source_id": "PM-266.2"
      },
      "evidence": [

          "anchor": "stmt-lem-contraction",
          "exact_quote": "Let $A$ be a balanced exponent matrix with $p$ rows and $q\\leq6$ columns, and suppose row $i$ has order one. The analogous relations hold for columns by transposition.",

      "exact_statement": "Let $A$ be a balanced exponent matrix with $p$ rows and $q\\leq6$ columns, and suppose row $i$ has order one.       "conclusion": {
        "anchor": "stmt-lem-partitions",
        "exact_quote": "Let $a_1,\\ldots,a_n\\in\\mathbb Z^6$ be balanced charges satisfying $\\sum_{j=1}^n a_j=0$, and let $A$ be the matrix with these columns. For a set partition $\\mathcal S$ of $\\{1,\\ldots,n\\}$, form $A_{\\mathcal S}$ by replacing the columns in each block $S\\in\\mathcal S$ by their sum $\\sum_{j\\in S}a_j$. Then $$\\left\\langle\n    \\mathop{\\mathrm{Re}}\\prod_{j=1}^n g_{\\widetilde H}(a_j)\n  \\right\\rangle_H\n  =\\sum_{\\substack{\\mathcal S\\text{ a set partition of }\\{1,\\ldots,n\\}\\\\\n                    |\\mathcal S|\\leq6}}\n       \\frac{(6)_{|\\mathcal S|}}{6^n}\\,\n       m_H(A_{\\mathcal S}).\n  \\label{eq:injective-partitions}$$ The ordering of the blocks of $\\mathcal S$ is immaterial.",
        "source_id": "PM-266.2"
      },
      "evidence": [

          "anchor": "stmt-lem-partitions",
          "exact_quote": "Let $a_1,\\ldots,a_n\\in\\mathbb Z^6$ be balanced charges satisfying $\\sum_{j=1}^n a_j=0$, and let $A$ be the matrix with these columns.           "source_id": "PM-266.2"
        }

        "exact_quote": "Suppose that $H$ and its entrywise square $H^{\\circ2}$ are Hadamard matrices of order six.         "anchor": "stmt-lem-basic",
        "exact_quote": "For each fixed reference basis the following identities hold. First, $$\\label{eq:order-two}\n g_s(d)=0\\quad(d\\in D),\n \\qquad\n \\sum_{s\\ne r}g_s(a)=0\\quad(\\mathop{\\mathrm{ord}}(a)=2).$$ For arbitrary charges $a,b$ and each coordinate $i$, $$\\label{eq:same-matrix}\n \\sum_{j=0}^5 g_s(a+e_i-e_j)g_s(b-e_i+e_j)=g_s(a+b).$$ The same identity holds with both shifts reversed. For distinct external bases $s,t\\ne r$, $$\\label{eq:different-matrices}\n \\sum_{d\\in D}g_s(a+d)g_t(b-d)=0.$$",
        "source_id": "PM-266.2"
      },
      "evidence": [

For distinct external bases $s,t\\ne r$, $$\\label{eq:different-matrices}\n \\sum_{d\\in D}g_s(a+d)g_t(b-d)=0.$$",

      "conclusion": {

        "exact_quote": "For balanced $a,v$, put $\\mathbf b=(a,-v,v-a)$.         "exact_quote": "Under <a href=\"#eq:all-M\" data-reference-type=\"eqref\" data-reference=\"eq:all-M\">[eq:all-M]</a>, the following operations and identities are valid.*\n\n1.  *A common coordinate permutation of all charges, simultaneous negation of all charges, a simultaneous reordering of factors and their labels, and an arbitrary injective renaming of the used labels preserve <a href=\"#eq:mixed-moment\" data-reference-type=\"eqref\" data-reference=\"eq:mixed-moment\">[eq:mixed-moment]</a>. A zero charge factor may be omitted. Coordinates on which every charge is zero may also be omitted, with the remaining coordinates interpreted as a uniform injection into the six row positions.*\n\n2.  *The moment vanishes if any charge has order one or is a coordinate permutation of $\\alpha$.*\n\n\n3.  *Suppose that $a_j$ has order two and its label $s_j$ occurs only once. Let $S$ be the set of distinct used labels and $l=|S|$. For each $t\\in S\\setminus\\{s_j\\}$, let $\\mathbf s^{j\\to t}$ be obtained from $\\mathbf s$ by replacing $s_j$ with $t$. Then $$\\label{eq:singleton-reduction}\n     \\mathcal M(\\mathbf s;\\mathbf a)\n     =-\\frac1{7-l}\\sum_{t\\in S\\setminus\\{s_j\\}}\n           \\mathcal M(\\mathbf s^{j\\to t};\\mathbf a).$$*\n\n\n4.  *If all labels are equal, the moment is a linear combination of the pair moments <a href=\"#eq:average-pair-moment\" data-reference-type=\"eqref\" data-reference=\"eq:average-pair-moment\">[eq:average-pair-moment]</a>. More precisely, let $A$ have columns $a_1,\\ldots,a_n$, after removing its zero rows. For a set partition $\\mathcal P$ of $\\{1,\\ldots,n\\}$, let $A_{\\mathcal P}$ have one column $\\sum_{j\\in B}a_j$ for each block $B\\in\\mathcal P$. Then $$\\label{eq:single-label-partitions}\n     \\mathcal M((s,\\ldots,s);\\mathbf a)\n     =\\sum_{\\substack{\\mathcal P\\text{ a set partition}\\\\|\\mathcal P|\\le6}}\n           \\frac{(6)_{|\\mathcal P|}}{6^n}\\,m(A_{\\mathcal P}),$$ where $(6)_k=6\\cdot5\\cdots(6-k+1)$. Zero rows or columns in $A_{\\mathcal P}$ may be deleted as in Section <a href=\"#sec:pair\" data-reference-type=\"ref\" data-reference=\"sec:pair\">3</a>.*\n\n\n\n5.  *For balanced $a,b,c$ with $a+b+c=0$, $$\\begin{aligned}\n     &\\sum_{j=0}^5\n     \\mathcal M\\bigl((s,s,u);\n           (a+e_i-e_j,b-e_i+e_j,c)\\bigr)\n       =\\mathcal M\\bigl((s,u);(a+b,c)\\bigr),\n           \\label{eq:mixed-same}\\\\\n     &\\sum_{d\\in D}\n     \\mathcal M\\bigl((s,t,u);(a+d,b-d,c)\\bigr)=0\n           \\qquad(s\\ne t).\n           \\label{eq:mixed-different}\n     \n    \\end{aligned}$$ Equation <a href=\"#eq:mixed-same\" data-reference-type=\"eqref\" data-reference=\"eq:mixed-same\">[eq:mixed-same]</a> also holds with the signs of both shifts reversed. If a rational matrix $P\\in\\mathbb Q^{N\\times N}$ satisfies $$P_{F,F}=I,\\qquad P_{*,J}=0,\\qquad WP=0,$$ then every real vector $\\mathbf m$ satisfying $W\\mathbf m=0$ obeys $\\mathbf m=P\\mathbf m$. <a id="pm-s2"></a>
## 2. Definitions, validity and bounds

- **complex Hadamard matrix of order six** ([source](#eq:g-definition)): A complex Hadamard matrix of order six is a matrix $H\in\mathbb T^{6\times6}$ with $H^*H=6I$, where $\mathbb T=\{z\in\mathbb C:\|z\|=1\}$.
- **Hadamard equivalence** ([source](#eq:cubic-matrix)): Here two Hadamard matrices are equivalent if they differ by row and column permutations and by multiplication of individual rows and columns by elements of $\mathbb T$.
- **mutually unbiased bases** ([source](#pm-h-3)): Two orthonormal bases in $\mathbb C^d$ are mutually unbiased if every inner product between their vectors has modulus $d^{-1/2}$.
- **maximum unbiased family size N(6)** ([source](#pm-h-3)): Write $N(6)$ for the maximum size of a mutually unbiased family in $\mathbb C^6$.
- **exponent matrix** ([source](#pm-h-7)): An *exponent matrix* is an integer matrix $A=(A_{uv})$ whose row sums and column sums are all zero.

<a id="pm-s3"></a>
## 3. Proof roadmap

- **step-contraction** [claim](#stmt-lem-contraction) / [proof](#proof-stmt-lem-contraction): Fix the other rows and use column orthogonality, averaging over unused row labels. -> Linear relation among injective moments.
- **step-partitions** [claim](#stmt-lem-partitions) / [proof](#proof-stmt-lem-partitions): Group sampled column choices by equality pattern. -> Expansion of character products into moments.
- **step-pair-certificate** [claim](#stmt-prop-pair-certificate) / [proof](#proof-stmt-prop-pair-certificate): Expand both squares, reduce by contractions and verify every coefficient vanishes in exact arithmetic. -> Vanishing sum of nonnegative averages.
- **step-dichotomy** [claim](#stmt-cor-dichotomy) / [proof](#proof-stmt-prop-pair-certificate-2): Nonnegative summands vanish, so squared row characters vanish and the entrywise square is Hadamard. -> Dichotomy between vanishing and Hadamard entrywise square.
- **step-cubic-classification** [claim](#stmt-prop-cubic-classification) / [proof](#proof-stmt-prop-cubic-classification): Newton identities on row ratios, common support argument, dimension argument excluding a block form, then a five-cycle. -> Equivalence to the cubic matrix.
- **step-fourier-theorem** [claim](#stmt-thm-fourier) / [proof](#proof-stmt-prop-cubic-classification-2): Contradiction combining the dichotomy with the classification. -> Fourier vanishing off the cubic class.
- **step-basic-identities** [claim](#stmt-lem-basic) / [proof](#proof-stmt-lem-basic): Projector identity from orthogonal traceless diagonal spaces and column orthogonality. -> Character identities of a complete family.
- **step-cubic-symmetry** [claim](#stmt-lem-cubic-symmetry) / [proof](#proof-stmt-lem-cubic-symmetry): Count normalized symmetries via the five-cycle structure and show their row permutations are distinct. -> Symmetry extending every row permutation.
- **step-cubic-exclusion** [claim](#stmt-prop-cubic-exclusion) / [proof](#proof-stmt-lem-cubic-symmetry-2): Orbit-average linear relations give a negative value for a nonnegative expectation. -> No Hadamard entrywise square among transitions.
- **step-gram** [claim](#stmt-lem-gram) / [proof](#proof-stmt-lem-gram): Average over the shared column and use label exchangeability. -> Gram entries as mixed moments.
- **step-mixed-reductions** [claim](#stmt-lem-mixed-reductions) / [proof](#proof-stmt-lem-mixed-reductions): Symmetry, orthogonality, singleton label replacement and partition expansion. -> Reduction rules for mixed moments.
- **step-exact-substitutions** [claim](#stmt-lem-exact-elimination) / [proof](#proof-stmt-lem-exact-elimination): Modular pivots then an exact rational identity certify substitutions. -> Valid substitution rules over the reals.
- **step-mixed-certificate** [claim](#stmt-prop-mixed-certificate) / [proof](#proof-stmt-prop-mixed-certificate): Expand the Young-symmetrized forms, substitute, and bound residual coordinates by one. -> Negative upper bound for a nonnegative form.
- **step-no-complete-family** [claim](#stmt-thm-main) / [proof](#proof-stmt-prop-mixed-certificate-2): Dichotomy plus cubic exclusion leaves the vanishing case, contradicted by the certificate. -> No seven mutually unbiased bases.

<a id="pm-s4"></a>
## 4. Engineering Triptych

### Panel 1 - epistemic contract

- **inv-cubic-matrix-both-hadamard**: The explicit cubic matrix T built from the five-cycle exponent pattern is Hadamard, and so is its entrywise square. In exponent form: for every pair of distinct rows, each residue modulo three occurs equally often among the column differences, and also among the doubled differences.
- **inv-cubic-cube-root-counts**: For a balanced charge b, the normalized character of T satisfies 6 g_T(b) = N_0 + N_1 omega + N_2 omega^2, where N_m counts the columns k whose exponent sum of b_i t_ik is congruent to m modulo three. The counts for the listed orbit representatives agree with the source table.

### Panel 2 - negative knowledge

- **anti-vanishing-on-cubic-class**: Assuming the character sums at permutations of alpha vanish for every order-six Hadamard matrix, including the cubic one. -> The vanishing statement is proved only outside the cubic equivalence class, so for matrices equivalent to T the conclusion is not guaranteed by the theorem.
- **anti-inexact-arithmetic-certificate**: Re-running the finite certificate checks in floating point and trusting the outcome as a proof. -> Rounding can hide a nonzero coefficient or a sign error, so equality and strict negativity are no longer certified.

### Panel 3 - reference implementation and oracle

Applicability: `constructive`. The cubic matrix T and its character counts are finite explicit constructions: the source gives the exponent array and a summation rule, so the Hadamard property and the cube-root counts can be computed exactly. The deep theorems of the paper (existence-type exclusion and the full certificate) are not re-run here.

```python
# INPUTS: one line on stdin: either the word hadamard, or six integers b_0..b_5 forming a balanced charge.
# PRECONDITIONS: for charge input the six integers sum to zero.
# OUTPUTS: for hadamard, the line 'hadamard 1' if T and its entrywise square pass the exact row test, else 'hadamard 0'; for a charge, three counts N0 N1 N2.
# TIME: constant, a few hundred elementary operations.
# SPACE: constant.
# ARITHMETIC: exact integers, exponents reduced modulo three; no floating point.
# FAILURE: malformed input raises an exception and the process exits with nonzero status.
import sys

def t(i, j):
    if i == 0 or j == 0 or i == j:
        return 0
    return 1 if (i-j)%5 in (1,4) else -1

def main():
    s = sys.stdin.read().split()
    if s[0] == 'hadamard':
        ok = 1
        for p in (1, 2):
            for i in range(6):
                for j in range(i+1, 6):
                    c = [0, 0, 0]
                    for k in range(6):
                        c[(p*(t(i,k)-t(j,k)))%3] += 1
                    if c[0] != c[1] or c[1] != c[2]:
                        ok = 0
        print('hadamard', ok)
        return
    b = [int(x) for x in s]
    N = [0, 0, 0]
    for k in range(6):
        N[sum(b[i]*t(i,k) for i in range(6))%3] += 1
    print(N[0], N[1], N[2])

main()
```

## 5. Domain engineering contracts

| domain | applies | strength | maturity | basis | why |
|---|---|---|---|---|---|
| software | True | moderate | adaptable | stated | The paper ships a complete exact verifier as a standalone program with a wrapper. Anyone can re-run it and compare the printed table sizes and coefficients against the published ones. This is a reproducibility artefact for the two certificates, not a general library. |
| games_simulation | False | none | not_applicable | inferred | The results concern exact algebraic identities and nonexistence of bases. There is no dynamics, rendering or stochastic simulation. The random sampling over injections is a proof device, not something to simulate. |
| tools | True | weak | adaptable | inferred | The exact-elimination procedure and the exact Schur-complement positivity check can be reused as a certificate checker for similar moment or sum-of-squares identities. The paper itself only applies them to its two certificates. |
| algorithms | True | moderate | implementable | inferred | Two finite procedures are explicit. The first finds candidate substitutions by modular elimination and verifies them by exact integer multiplication. The second certifies positive definiteness through successive exact Schur complements. Both are deterministic and exact. |
| data_structures | False | none | not_applicable | inferred | No new data structure is proposed. Canonical representatives of exponent arrays are only a naming device for moment coordinates inside the verifier. |
| hardware_accelerators | False | none | not_applicable | inferred | Nothing in the paper addresses parallel or hardware execution. Runtime and throughput are not analysed, so no accelerator contract can be drawn. |
| crypto_security | False | none | not_applicable | inferred | No cryptographic primitive, hardness assumption or adversary model appears. Rational arithmetic is used only to certify identities. |
| machine_learning | False | none | not_applicable | inferred | The semidefinite and moment viewpoint is cited as background for positive forms, but nothing is trained or learned. The forms have fixed integer data. |
| optimisation_operations_research | False | none | not_applicable | inferred | The paper does not solve an optimisation problem. The Gram matrices are fixed, supplied integer data, so no solver, objective or tolerance is involved. |
| distributed_systems | False | none | not_applicable | inferred | The verification is a single sequential exact computation. No communication, consensus or fault model is present. |
| other | False | none | not_applicable | inferred | The physical context is quantum state determination with mutually unbiased measurements. The paper gives only a mathematical nonexistence bound for complete families, which is not an engineering construction. |

<a id="pm-s6"></a>
## 6. Applicability limits and search

- **limit-cubic-class-excluded** (hypothesis): The Fourier-vanishing conclusion is proved only for order-six complex Hadamard matrices that are not equivalent to the cubic matrix T. For matrices in that class the theorem gives no vanishing. Blocked use: ['Assuming the permuted-alpha character sums vanish for every order-six Hadamard matrix, including the cubic class', 'Applying the vanishing in classification software without first testing equivalence to T']
- **limit-order-six-balanced** (hypothesis): The characters, moments and contraction identities are set up for order six and balanced integer charges whose coordinates sum to zero. The seven-row relation uses the specific count of six available rows. Blocked use: ['Extending the vanishing claim to other orders', 'Evaluating g_H at charges that are not balanced and expecting phase invariance']
- **limit-n6-upper-bound-only** (formal_scope): The complete-family theorem excludes seven mutually unbiased bases and, with the completion theorem, only gives an upper bound. A sharper value is cited from a companion paper under its own arithmetic conditions. Blocked use: ['Reading the bound as the exact maximum number of mutually unbiased bases in dimension six', 'Planning measurement schemes that assume four or five such bases exist']
- **limit-single-matrix-not-basis-count** (formal_scope): The Fourier theorem concerns each individual Hadamard matrix; its content is distinct from a bound on the number of bases. Blocked use: ['Deducing a bound on the number of bases from the single-matrix vanishing alone']
- **limit-substitution-relies-on-selected-equations** (approximation): The exact substitution lemma certifies only the retained equations. The modular pivot count is not asserted to be the full rational rank, and no completeness of the enumerated relations is assumed. ### Formal statement (Lean 4)

Corpus status: formalized in the source corpus (toolchain `leanprover/lean4:v4.34.1`, Mathlib `d13f23b723b8a846827a245b89c10fc7d3f11612`). Not re-checked here; the statements give the exact quantifiers and hypotheses, not a proof.

Scope note of family 266 (`lean/docs/266.md`, verbatim):

> The following describes the scope of the Lean formalization related to the following accompanying paper(s):
>
> - [The maximum number of mutually unbiased bases in dimension six](../../preprints/The-maximum-number-of-mutually-unbiased-bases-in-dimension-six-September-24-2026/The-maximum-number-of-mutually-unbiased-bases-in-dimension-six-September-24-2026.pdf)
> - [Exact Fourier certificates for complex Hadamard matrices of order six](../../preprints/Exact-Fourier-certificates-for-complex-Hadamard-matrices-of-order-six-September-24-2026/Exact-Fourier-certificates-for-complex-Hadamard-matrices-of-order-six-September-24-2026.pdf)
>
> ## Scope
>
> The paper claims that at most three mutually unbiased orthonormal bases exist in $\mathbb C^6$. The linked formalization proves a weaker family bound: every family in its mutually unbiased bases model has at most five members. It also proves a Fourier character-sum vanishing statement for order-six complex Hadamard matrices not equivalent to the Tao matrix, uniformly over coordinate permutations.
>
> The selected statement does not establish the paper's upper bound of three or its computer-assisted exclusion of four arbitrary bases.
>
> The linked formalization proves a cancellation lemma used in the order-six complex Hadamard analysis. Let $H$ and its entrywise square both be complex Hadamard matrices, and fix two distinct rows. If the cubes of their six entrywise ratios take only two distinct values, then the sum of the row-ratio terms over either specified cube fiber is zero.
>
> This is a supporting Fourier cancellation statement. The paper's full character-sum vanishing theorem and its mutually unbiased bases bound are outside this selected statement.

Comparator source `HadamardCubeFiber.lean` (1084 bytes, sha256 `9f412781be8b38c88a6efb3a4528d4c990071a233cd931612d4710213d6366a1`), verbatim:

```lean
import Mathlib

namespace OAI

/-! Cancellation of row ratios on a prescribed cubic fibre. -/

open scoped BigOperators Matrix

namespace HadamardSix

abbrev Index := Fin 6
abbrev Matrix6 := Matrix Index Index ℂ

def Unimodular (H : Matrix6) : Prop := ∀ i j, ‖H i j‖ = 1

def IsComplexHadamard (H : Matrix6) : Prop :=
  Unimodular H ∧ H.conjTranspose * H = (6 : ℂ) • (1 : Matrix6)

def entrywiseSquare (H : Matrix6) : Matrix6 := fun i j => H i j ^ 2

noncomputable def cubeFiber (v : Index → ℂ) (z : ℂ) : Finset Index := by
  classical
  exact Finset.univ.filter (fun i => v i ^ 3 = z)

noncomputable def rowRatio (H : Matrix6) (i j : Index) : Index → ℂ :=
  fun k => H i k * star (H j k)

lemma rowRatio_cubeFiber_sum_zero {H : Matrix6} (hH : IsComplexHadamard H)
    (hs : IsComplexHadamard (entrywiseSquare H)) {i j : Index} (hij : i ≠ j)
    (a b : ℂ) (hne : a ≠ b)
    (hab : ∀ k, rowRatio H i j k ^ 3 = a ∨ rowRatio H i j k ^ 3 = b) :
    ∑ k ∈ cubeFiber (rowRatio H i j) a, H i k * star (H j k) = 0 := by
  sorry

end HadamardSix

end OAI
```

<a id="pm-s7"></a>
## 7. Complete mathematical paper


We prove the Fourier-vanishing conjecture of Matolcsi, Ruzsa, and Weiner: every complex Hadamard matrix of order six outside Tao's cubic equivalence class has a vanishing character sum at every permutation of $`(1,1,1,-1,-1,-1)`$. We also give an exact certificate excluding seven mutually unbiased bases in ℂ<sup>6</sup>; by Weiner's completion theorem, this yields an upper bound of five. Both certificates use only integer and rational arithmetic, and the complete verifier is included.

<a id="pm-h-1"></a>
#### Statements index

| anchor | kind | label | number | first sentence |
|---|---|---|---|---|
| [stmt-thm-fourier](#stmt-thm-fourier) | theorem | `thm:fourier` | 1 | Let $H$ be a complex Hadamard matrix of order six. |
| [stmt-thm-main](#stmt-thm-main) | theorem | `thm:main` | 2 | There do not exist seven orthonormal bases $B_r=(b_{r,0},\ldots,b_{r,5})$ of $\mathbb C^6$, $1\le r\le7$, such that |
| [stmt-cor-dichotomy](#stmt-cor-dichotomy) | corollary | `cor:dichotomy` | 3 | For every $6\times6$ Hadamard matrix $H$, at least one of the following conditions holds:* |
| [stmt-lem-contraction](#stmt-lem-contraction) | lemma | `lem:contraction` | 4 | Let $A$ be a balanced exponent matrix with $p$ rows and $q\leq6$ columns, and suppose row $i$ has order one. |
| [stmt-lem-partitions](#stmt-lem-partitions) | lemma | `lem:partitions` | 5 | Let $a_1,\ldots,a_n\in\mathbb Z^6$ be balanced charges satisfying $\sum_{j=1}^n a_j=0$, and let $A$ be the matrix with these columns. |
| [stmt-prop-pair-certificate](#stmt-prop-pair-certificate) | proposition | `prop:pair-certificate` | 6 | For every $6\times6$ Hadamard matrix $H$ and for each $q\in\{q_{\mathrm{cross}},q_{\mathrm{same}}\}$, |
| [stmt-prop-cubic-classification](#stmt-prop-cubic-classification) | proposition | `prop:cubic-classification` | 7 | Suppose that $H$ and its entrywise square $H^{\circ2}$ are Hadamard matrices of order six. |
| [stmt-lem-basic](#stmt-lem-basic) | lemma | `lem:basic` | 8 | For each fixed reference basis the following identities hold. |
| [stmt-prop-cubic-exclusion](#stmt-prop-cubic-exclusion) | proposition | `prop:cubic-exclusion` | 9 | In a set of seven mutually unbiased bases in $\mathbb C^6$, no transition Hadamard matrix $H$ can have $H^{\circ2}$ Hadamard.* |
| [stmt-lem-cubic-symmetry](#stmt-lem-cubic-symmetry) | lemma | `lem:cubic-symmetry` | 10 | Every permutation of the rows of $T$ can be completed to a symmetry of $T$ by column permutations, row and column phase factors, and, if necessary, complex conjugation. |
| [stmt-lem-gram](#stmt-lem-gram) | lemma | `lem:gram` | 11 | For balanced $a,v$, put $\mathbf b=(a,-v,v-a)$. |
| [stmt-lem-mixed-reductions](#stmt-lem-mixed-reductions) | lemma | `lem:mixed-reductions` | 12 | Under [eq:all-M], the following operations and identities are valid.* |
| [stmt-prop-mixed-certificate](#stmt-prop-mixed-certificate) | proposition | `prop:mixed-certificate` | 13 | Each matrix $Z_\lambda$ specified above is positive definite. |
| [stmt-lem-exact-elimination](#stmt-lem-exact-elimination) | lemma | `lem:exact-elimination` | 14 | Let $W\in\mathbb Z^{r\times N}$ consist of selected moment equations after their denominators have been cleared. |

<a id="sec:introduction"></a>
### Introduction

<a id="eq:g-definition"></a>
A complex Hadamard matrix of order six is a matrix $H\in\mathbb T^{6\times6}$ with $H^*H=6I$, where $\mathbb T=\{z\in\mathbb C:|z|=1\}$. Its columns become an orthonormal basis after normalization by $1/\sqrt6$. The same columns are also points of the torus $\mathbb T^6$, and their character sums record constraints that orthogonality places on their phases. We study the normalized sums $$\label{eq:g-definition}
 g_H(a)=\frac16\sum_{k=0}^5\prod_{i=0}^5H(i,k)^{a_i},
 \qquad a\in\mathbb Z^6,\quad\sum_{i=0}^5a_i=0.$$ The balancing condition makes these sums invariant under multiplying individual columns by phases. Write $e_0,\ldots,e_5$ for the standard coordinate vectors. Orthogonality gives $g_H(e_i-e_j)=0$ for $i\ne j$. A more restrictive vanishing at the charge $$\alpha=(1,1,1,-1,-1,-1)$$ was conjectured by Matolcsi, Ruzsa, and Weiner (Matolcsi, Ruzsa, and Weiner 2013, Conjecture 2.3) <a id="pm-cite-1"></a>[[MatolcsiRuzsaWeiner2013](#ref-MatolcsiRuzsaWeiner2013)]. It links a degree-six character to the structure of an individual Hadamard matrix, without assuming that the matrix belongs to a larger family of unbiased bases.

<a id="eq:cubic-matrix"></a>
The conjecture has one exceptional equivalence class. Here two Hadamard matrices are equivalent if they differ by row and column permutations and by multiplication of individual rows and columns by elements of $\mathbb T$. Put $\omega=\exp(2\pi\mathrm i/3)$, and define $T=(\omega^{t_{ij}})_{i,j=0}^5$ by $$\label{eq:cubic-matrix}
 t_{ij}=\begin{cases}
 0,&i=0\text{ or }j=0\text{ or }i=j,\\
 1,&i,j\in\{1,\ldots,5\},\quad i-j\equiv\pm1\pmod5,\\
 -1,&\text{otherwise}.
 \end{cases}$$ This is the cubic matrix in Tao’s construction (Tao 2004, 254) <a id="pm-cite-2"></a>[[Tao2004](#ref-Tao2004)]; the same representative already appears in Moorhouse’s study of $2$-transitive complex Hadamard matrices (Moorhouse 2001, Example 1.1) <a id="pm-cite-3"></a>[[Moorhouse2001](#ref-Moorhouse2001)]. Its five noninitial indices form a cycle: adjacent indices carry the exponent $1$ and nonadjacent distinct indices carry $-1$. The matrix and its entrywise square are both Hadamard.

<a id="stmt-thm-fourier"></a>
<div id="thm:fourier" class="theorem">

**Theorem 1** (Fourier vanishing). *Let $H$ be a complex Hadamard matrix of order six. If $H$ is not equivalent to the cubic matrix $T$ in <a href="#eq:cubic-matrix" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="eq:cubic-matrix">[eq:cubic-matrix]</a>, then $$g_H(\pi\alpha)=0\qquad\text{for every coordinate permutation }\pi.$$*

</div>

Theorem <a href="#thm:fourier" data-reference-type="ref" data-reference="thm:fourier">1</a> proves the conjecture for arbitrary complex Hadamard matrices. The normalization in <a href="#eq:g-definition" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="eq:g-definition">[eq:g-definition]</a> divides the character sums used in (Matolcsi, Ruzsa, and Weiner 2013) <a id="pm-cite-4"></a>[[MatolcsiRuzsaWeiner2013](#ref-MatolcsiRuzsaWeiner2013)] by six and therefore leaves the vanishing assertion unchanged. Multiplying rows by phases multiplies each character by a unit scalar; together with the balancing condition, this shows that the assertion respects the stated equivalence relation.

<a id="pm-h-3"></a>
#### Fourier methods and mutually unbiased bases

Two orthonormal bases in $\mathbb C^d$ are mutually unbiased if every inner product between their vectors has modulus $d^{-1/2}$. Such bases describe complementary measurements and arise in quantum state determination (Ivanović 1981; Wootters and Fields 1989) <a id="pm-cite-5"></a>[[Ivanovic1981](#ref-Ivanovic1981), [WoottersFields1989](#ref-WoottersFields1989)]. A family contains at most $d+1$ bases, and complete families attaining that bound exist in prime-power dimensions (Wootters and Fields 1989) <a id="pm-cite-6"></a>[[WoottersFields1989](#ref-WoottersFields1989)]. The first dimension outside that construction is six. Write $N(6)$ for the maximum size of a mutually unbiased family in $\mathbb C^6$.

Fixing one basis identifies every other unbiased basis with a complex Hadamard matrix. Matolcsi developed a Fourier and Delsarte approach to the resulting constraints on torus points (Matolcsi 2012) <a id="pm-cite-7"></a>[[Matolcsi2012](#ref-Matolcsi2012)]. Matolcsi, Ruzsa, and Weiner developed identities for the associated character sums and formulated the vanishing conjecture addressed here (Matolcsi, Ruzsa, and Weiner 2013) <a id="pm-cite-8"></a>[[MatolcsiRuzsaWeiner2013](#ref-MatolcsiRuzsaWeiner2013)]. Maxwell and Brierley proved that vanishing for the Karlsson family (Maxwell and Brierley 2015, Theorem 4.1) <a id="pm-cite-9"></a>[[MaxwellBrierley2015](#ref-MaxwellBrierley2015)]. Theorem <a href="#thm:fourier" data-reference-type="ref" data-reference="thm:fourier">1</a> removes the family restriction. The exceptional matrix is usually denoted $S_6$ in the Hadamard literature (Tadej and Życzkowski 2006, sec. 5.6.4) <a id="pm-cite-10"></a>[[TadejZyczkowski2006](#ref-TadejZyczkowski2006)]; Section <a href="#sec:cubic" data-reference-type="ref" data-reference="sec:cubic">4</a> gives the precise identification with <a href="#eq:cubic-matrix" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="eq:cubic-matrix">[eq:cubic-matrix]</a>.

The structural step has a separate antecedent. Lisoněk introduced *S-Hadamard matrices*, for which both $H$ and $H^{\circ2}$ are Hadamard (Lisoněk 2019, Definition 2.1) <a id="pm-cite-11"></a>[[Lisonek2019](#ref-Lisonek2019)]. Phangara conjectured that every order-six S-Hadamard matrix is equivalent to the cubic example (Phangara 2026, Conjecture 3.1.6) <a id="pm-cite-12"></a>[[Phangara2026](#ref-Phangara2026)]. Proposition <a href="#prop:cubic-classification" data-reference-type="ref" data-reference="prop:cubic-classification">7</a> proves this assertion. The uniqueness of the cube-root class itself appears in the Butson classification of Lampio, Östergård, and Szöllősi (Lampio, Östergård, and Szöllősi 2020, Theorem 4.1 and Table 2) <a id="pm-cite-13"></a>[[LampioOstergardSzollosi2020](#ref-LampioOstergardSzollosi2020)] and in Liang, Chen, Long, and Qiu (Liang et al. 2021, Lemma 10) <a id="pm-cite-14"></a>[[LiangChenLongQiu2021](#ref-LiangChenLongQiu2021)]. The additional step here derives cube-root entries from the two Hadamard conditions on arbitrary phases.

There is also a substantial history of positive certificates for the MUB problem. Brierley and Weigert formulated polynomial and semidefinite approaches (Brierley and Weigert 2010) <a id="pm-cite-15"></a>[[BrierleyWeigert2010SDP](#ref-BrierleyWeigert2010SDP)]. Matolcsi and Weiner strengthened the Delsarte witness method for prescribed Hadamard families (Matolcsi and Weiner 2015) <a id="pm-cite-16"></a>[[MatolcsiWeiner2015](#ref-MatolcsiWeiner2015)], and Kolountzakis, Matolcsi, and Weiner developed the positive-definite-function approach on compact groups (Kolountzakis, Matolcsi, and Weiner 2018) <a id="pm-cite-17"></a>[[KolountzakisMatolcsiWeiner2018](#ref-KolountzakisMatolcsiWeiner2018)]. Gribling and Polak applied permutation symmetry to positive moment matrices in a tracial noncommutative formulation of the MUB problem (Gribling and Polak 2024) <a id="pm-cite-18"></a>[[GriblingPolak2024](#ref-GriblingPolak2024)]. Our finite invariant-moment certificates belong to this line of work. Their specific character identities and shared-column test functions are described below.

Bandeira, Doppelbauer, and Kunisky proved that positive-definite polynomials of degree at most six in the entries of a single unitary transition matrix and their conjugates cannot improve the seven-basis bound in dimension six (Bandeira, Doppelbauer, and Kunisky 2022, Theorem 1.10) <a id="pm-cite-19"></a>[[BandeiraDoppelbauerKunisky2022](#ref-BandeiraDoppelbauerKunisky2022)]. That result concerns its specified single-matrix relaxation. The mixed certificate here uses products of character sums from several transitions and relations of higher total order.

The individual-matrix statement also supplies an input to a second exact certificate within this paper. It yields the following complete-family obstruction.

<a id="stmt-thm-main"></a>
<div id="thm:main" class="theorem">

**Theorem 2** (No complete family). *There do not exist seven orthonormal bases $B_r=(b_{r,0},\ldots,b_{r,5})$ of $\mathbb C^6$, $1\le r\le7$, such that $$\bigl|\langle b_{r,i},b_{s,j}\rangle\bigr|^2=\frac16
 \qquad(r\ne s,\ 0\le i,j\le5).$$*

</div>

By Weiner’s completion theorem, any $d$ mutually unbiased bases in $\mathbb C^d$ extend to a complete family (Weiner 2013, Corollary 3.4) <a id="pm-cite-20"></a>[[Weiner2013](#ref-Weiner2013)]. Theorem <a href="#thm:main" data-reference-type="ref" data-reference="thm:main">2</a> therefore gives $N(6)\le5$. Under its stated binary64 arithmetic and compiler conditions and complete-execution certificate contract, the companion paper (OpenAI 2026, Theorem 1.1, Section 7 and Appendix A) <a id="pm-cite-21"></a>[[OpenAI2026MUBMaximum](#ref-OpenAI2026MUBMaximum)] gives the sharper value $N(6)=3$. The Fourier theorem concerns each individual Hadamard matrix; its content is distinct from a bound on the number of bases. For transitions in MUB triplets, Matolcsi, Matszangosz, Varga, and Weiner conjecture both this vanishing and a further condition coupling characters at $3\alpha$ for $H$ and $H^*$ (Matolcsi et al. 2026, Conjecture 2) <a id="pm-cite-22"></a>[[MatolcsiEtAl2025](#ref-MatolcsiEtAl2025)]. Their route to $N(6)=3$ also uses a separate conjecture excluding the Szöllősi family from quadruplets (Matolcsi et al. 2026, Conjecture 3 and Proposition 4.2) <a id="pm-cite-23"></a>[[MatolcsiEtAl2025](#ref-MatolcsiEtAl2025)].

<a id="pm-h-4"></a>
#### Proof strategy

The main difficulty is to obtain a universal statement about phases from orthogonality without restricting the matrix to a parametrized family. We average monomials in its entries over distinct row and column indices. Summing a row with one exponent $+1$ and one exponent $-1$ against orthogonality expresses its moment in terms of moments with merged rows. A formal seven-row version has no unused physical row; it gives a homogeneous relation among six-row moments. Products of character sums expand into these injective moments by grouping column samples according to which indices coincide.

The first certificate adds an auxiliary absolute square to a product $|g_H(\alpha)g_H(q)|^2$ and proves that their finite average is zero. Every summand is nonnegative, so the product vanishes for every row ordering. Two choices of $q$ force a dichotomy: either all permuted $\alpha$ characters vanish, or the entrywise square $H^{\circ2}$ is Hadamard. In the latter case, Newton’s identities constrain every row ratio to two rotated triples of cube roots. An orthogonality argument then forces the whole dephased matrix to be cubic, and a five-cycle classifies it as $T$. This proves Theorem <a href="#thm:fourier" data-reference-type="ref" data-reference="thm:fourier">1</a>.

The complete-family obstruction requires more than the vanishing condition on one matrix. We first exclude a cubic transition matrix from such a family by an exact finite character calculation. For the remaining case, we average products of character sums over ordered basis pairs and triples. Sampling one additional column shared by all test functions expresses their Gram entries as combinations of three-factor moments. Six explicitly specified positive quadratic forms have a combined expansion whose constant term dominates, with negative sign, the sum of the absolute values of all other coefficients. Since every retained moment has absolute value at most one, this contradicts positivity.

The certificates are finite rational identities. Modular elimination locates candidate substitutions, and exact integer multiplication proves that they hold for every actual moment vector. The calculation does not require those moment values to be rational. Section <a href="#sec:verification" data-reference-type="ref" data-reference="sec:verification">7</a> explains this distinction and traces the verifier’s operations back to the sampled variables.

Section <a href="#sec:preliminaries" data-reference-type="ref" data-reference="sec:preliminaries">2</a> fixes the character notation. Section <a href="#sec:pair" data-reference-type="ref" data-reference="sec:pair">3</a> constructs the single-matrix certificate and its dichotomy. Section <a href="#sec:cubic" data-reference-type="ref" data-reference="sec:cubic">4</a> classifies the cubic alternative and proves Theorem <a href="#thm:fourier" data-reference-type="ref" data-reference="thm:fourier">1</a>. Section <a href="#sec:complete-family" data-reference-type="ref" data-reference="sec:complete-family">5</a> derives the complete-family identities and excludes a cubic pair. Section <a href="#sec:mixed" data-reference-type="ref" data-reference="sec:mixed">6</a> constructs the shared-column Gram certificate and proves Theorem <a href="#thm:main" data-reference-type="ref" data-reference="thm:main">2</a>. Section <a href="#sec:verification" data-reference-type="ref" data-reference="sec:verification">7</a> verifies both exact calculations, and Appendix <a href="#app:code" data-reference-type="ref" data-reference="app:code">8</a> prints the complete standard-library Python verifier, including all integer weights.

<a id="sec:preliminaries"></a>
### Characters and phase invariance

The Hermitian inner product is conjugate-linear in its first argument. We index the coordinates of $\mathbb C^6$ by $0,\ldots,5$, and write $e_0,\ldots,e_5$ for the standard coordinate vectors. For a square Hadamard matrix, $H^*H=6I$ also implies $HH^*=6I$. The equivalence relation and normalized character $g_H$ are those of Section <a href="#sec:introduction" data-reference-type="ref" data-reference="sec:introduction">1</a>. Every Hadamard matrix can be *dephased*, meaning that its first row and first column are all ones, by multiplying rows and columns by elements of $\mathbb T$.

A *charge* is a vector $a\in\mathbb Z^6$ with $\sum_i a_i=0$. Its order and character are $$\mathop{\mathrm{ord}}(a)=\sum_{a_i>0}a_i,
 \qquad x^a=\prod_{i=0}^5x_i^{a_i}\quad(x\in\mathbb T^6).$$ For the column characters in <a href="#eq:g-definition" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="eq:g-definition">[eq:g-definition]</a>, put $$D=\{e_i-e_j:0\le i,j\le5,\ i\ne j\}.$$ We retain $\alpha=(1,1,1,-1,-1,-1)$ from the introduction. In particular, $|g_H(a)|\le1$, $g_H(0)=1$, and $g_H(-a)=\overline{g_H(a)}$. A permutation $p$ acts by $(P_pa)_i=a_{p(i)}$. When we write a permuted charge, all coordinates are permuted together; we also abbreviate $P_pa$ as $pa$.

Balanced charges make $g_H$ invariant under column phases. Multiplying row $i$ by $u_i$ multiplies $g_H(a)$ by $u^a$. Thus products whose total charge is zero are unchanged by row phases as well. These elementary observations will justify dephasing and the invariant moments below.

<a id="sec:pair"></a>
### Injective moments and a dichotomy for one Hadamard matrix

For a matrix $H$ and a row permutation $\pi$, write $H^\pi(i,k)=H(\pi(i),k)$. The following dichotomy is the single-matrix step in the proof of Theorem <a href="#thm:fourier" data-reference-type="ref" data-reference="thm:fourier">1</a>.

<a id="stmt-cor-dichotomy"></a>
<div id="cor:dichotomy" class="corollary">

**Corollary 3** (Dichotomy). *For every $6\times6$ Hadamard matrix $H$, at least one of the following conditions holds:*

1.  *$g_{H^\pi}(\alpha)=0$ for every row permutation $\pi$;*

2.  *the entrywise square $H^{\circ2}$ is a Hadamard matrix.*

</div>

Condition (M) is the Fourier condition in Matolcsi, Ruzsa, and Weiner’s conjecture (Matolcsi, Ruzsa, and Weiner 2013, Conjecture 2.3) <a id="pm-cite-24"></a>[[MatolcsiRuzsaWeiner2013](#ref-MatolcsiRuzsaWeiner2013)]; Section <a href="#sec:cubic" data-reference-type="ref" data-reference="sec:cubic">4</a> will classify condition (C). We prove the dichotomy from an exact identity between averages of nonnegative quantities, using only orthogonality and unimodularity of one matrix.

<a id="pm-h-7"></a>
#### Injective moments

Fix a $6\times6$ Hadamard matrix $H$. An *exponent matrix* is an integer matrix $A=(A_{uv})$ whose row sums and column sums are all zero. For $A$ of size $p\times q$, with $p,q\leq6$, define <a id="eq:injective-moment"></a>$$m_H(A)=
  \mathbb E\,\mathop{\mathrm{Re}}\!\left[
    \prod_{u=0}^{p-1}\prod_{v=0}^{q-1}
      K\bigl(\rho(u),\kappa(v)\bigr)^{A_{uv}}
  \right].
  \label{eq:injective-moment}$$ Here $K$ is $H$ or $H^{\mathsf T}$ with probability $1/2$ each, $\rho:\{0,\ldots,p-1\}\to\{0,\ldots,5\}$ and $\kappa:\{0,\ldots,q-1\}\to\{0,\ldots,5\}$ are uniformly chosen injections, and these three choices are independent. Negative exponents are well defined because every matrix entry has modulus one. We set $m_H(\varnothing)=1$ for the empty exponent matrix. In particular, <a id="eq:injective-bound"></a>$$|m_H(A)|\leq1.
  \label{eq:injective-bound}$$

Permuting the rows or columns of $A$ leaves this expectation unchanged. Replacing $A$ by $-A$ conjugates the monomial and therefore leaves its real part unchanged. Also $m_H(A^{\mathsf T})=m_H(A)$: transposition exchanges the two injections and interchanges the equally weighted choices of $K$. Finally, a zero row or column can be deleted. Indeed, its factor is one, and deleting a coordinate of a uniformly chosen injection leaves a uniformly chosen injection on the remaining coordinates. These facts justify all the identifications of exponent matrices used below.

For a balanced integer row $a$, its order is $\mathop{\mathrm{ord}}(a)=\sum_{j:a_j>0}a_j$, consistently with the notation for charges. The following relation eliminates any row of order one.

<a id="stmt-lem-contraction"></a>
<div id="lem:contraction" class="lemma">

<a id="eq:injective-contraction"></a>
<a id="eq:seven-row-contraction"></a>
**Lemma 4** (Contraction of an order-one row). *Let $A$ be a balanced exponent matrix with $p$ rows and $q\leq6$ columns, and suppose row $i$ has order one. For $\ell\ne i$, let $A_{i\to\ell}$ be obtained by adding row $i$ to row $\ell$ and then deleting row $i$. If $p\leq6$, then $$(7-p)m_H(A)
    =-\sum_{\substack{0\leq\ell<p\\\ell\ne i}}
        m_H(A_{i\to\ell}).
  \label{eq:injective-contraction}$$ If $p=7$, the valid relation is instead $$\sum_{\substack{0\leq\ell<7\\\ell\ne i}}
      m_H(A_{i\to\ell})=0.
  \label{eq:seven-row-contraction}$$ Every moment in <a href="#eq:seven-row-contraction" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="eq:seven-row-contraction">[eq:seven-row-contraction]</a> has at most six rows; no moment with seven injectively sampled rows is defined or used. The analogous relations hold for columns by transposition.*

</div>

<details id="proof-stmt-lem-contraction"><summary>Proof of stmt-lem-contraction</summary>

*Proof.* Since row $i$ is balanced and has order one, it has a $+1$ in one column $v_+$, a $-1$ in another column $v_-$, and zeros elsewhere. Fix $K$, the column injection $\kappa$, and an injective assignment of the other $p-1$ rows. Write $M$ for the product of the factors contributed by those rows. The columns $\kappa(v_+)$ and $\kappa(v_-)$ are distinct, so column orthogonality gives $$M\sum_{t=0}^{5}
     K\bigl(t,\kappa(v_+)\bigr)
     \overline{K\bigl(t,\kappa(v_-)\bigr)}=0.$$ Both choices of $K$ are Hadamard matrices: for a square matrix, column orthogonality also gives row orthogonality.

When $p\leq6$, there are $7-p$ row labels not yet assigned. The sum over those labels is $7-p$ times the conditional expectation obtained by assigning row $i$ uniformly to an unused label. Averaging the already fixed assignments produces $(7-p)m_H(A)$. For each occupied label, say that of row $\ell$, the corresponding monomial adds the exponents of row $i$ to those of row $\ell$. Its average is $m_H(A_{i\to\ell})$. Taking real parts and averaging also over $K$ and $\kappa$ proves <a href="#eq:injective-contraction" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="eq:injective-contraction">[eq:injective-contraction]</a>. If a contraction creates a zero row or column, deletion is justified by the marginal argument preceding the lemma.

When $p=7$, the six other rows occupy all six available labels. There is no unused-label contribution. The same orthogonality equation is therefore precisely the sum of the six contractions in <a href="#eq:seven-row-contraction" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="eq:seven-row-contraction">[eq:seven-row-contraction]</a>. ◻

</details>

Repeatedly applying Lemma <a href="#lem:contraction" data-reference-type="ref" data-reference="lem:contraction">4</a> to rows or columns of order one expresses any moment with both dimensions at most six as a rational linear combination of the empty moment and moments whose nonzero rows and columns all have order at least two. Each contraction reduces the number of rows or columns, so this procedure terminates. Choosing representatives under the symmetries above introduces no additional mathematical relation.

<a id="pm-h-8"></a>
#### From products of characters to injective moments

For a permutation $\pi$ of $\{0,\ldots,5\}$, write $K^\pi(i,k)=K(\pi(i),k)$. We use the notation <a id="eq:pair-average"></a>$$\bigl\langle F(\widetilde H)\bigr\rangle_H
   =\frac{1}{2\cdot6!}
     \sum_{K\in(H,H^{\mathsf T})}\ \sum_{\pi\in S_6} F(K^\pi)
  \label{eq:pair-average}$$ for the average over the two orientations and all row permutations. The ordered pair $(H,H^{\mathsf T})$ in this formula is counted with multiplicity even when its entries coincide. Column permutations do not change $g_{\widetilde H}$ and need not be included.

Let $(6)_k=6\cdot5\cdots(6-k+1)$, with $(6)_0=1$.

<a id="stmt-lem-partitions"></a>
<div id="lem:partitions" class="lemma">

<a id="eq:injective-partitions"></a>
**Lemma 5** (Equality-pattern expansion). *Let $a_1,\ldots,a_n\in\mathbb Z^6$ be balanced charges satisfying $\sum_{j=1}^n a_j=0$, and let $A$ be the matrix with these columns. For a set partition $\mathcal S$ of $\{1,\ldots,n\}$, form $A_{\mathcal S}$ by replacing the columns in each block $S\in\mathcal S$ by their sum $\sum_{j\in S}a_j$. Then $$\left\langle
    \mathop{\mathrm{Re}}\prod_{j=1}^n g_{\widetilde H}(a_j)
  \right\rangle_H
  =\sum_{\substack{\mathcal S\text{ a set partition of }\{1,\ldots,n\}\\
                    |\mathcal S|\leq6}}
       \frac{(6)_{|\mathcal S|}}{6^n}\,
       m_H(A_{\mathcal S}).
  \label{eq:injective-partitions}$$ The ordering of the blocks of $\mathcal S$ is immaterial.*

</div>

<details id="proof-stmt-lem-partitions"><summary>Proof of stmt-lem-partitions</summary>

*Proof.* Expanding the product of characters samples $n$ columns independently and uniformly from the six columns of $\widetilde H$. Group the summands by their equality pattern: two positions belong to the same block precisely when their column labels coincide. For a specified partition with $k$ blocks, the distinct block labels have $(6)_k$ possible assignments, each with probability $6^{-n}$. Conditional on this pattern, the block labels form a uniform injection. Multiplying the factors with the same column label adds their exponents, giving $A_{\mathcal S}$. Averaging over the row permutation, the orientation, and the real part gives exactly its injective moment. Partitions with more than six blocks have no assignments and contribute nothing. ◻

</details>

The hypotheses on the charges ensure that every matrix in <a href="#eq:injective-partitions" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="eq:injective-partitions">[eq:injective-partitions]</a> has both row and column sums zero. Moreover, $\overline{g_H(a)}=g_H(-a)$, so absolute squares of products are covered by the lemma. A zero charge may be omitted before applying the expansion, since its factor is $g_H(0)=1$; equivalently, integrating out its independently sampled column does not change the distribution of the remaining columns.

For example, if $a$ is a balanced charge and $[a,-a]$ denotes the two-column exponent matrix, the two possible equality patterns give $$\left\langle |g_{\widetilde H}(a)|^2\right\rangle_H
 =\frac16+\frac56m_H([a,-a]).$$ The first term comes from equal sampled columns, where all exponents cancel; the second comes from two distinct columns. The certificate below uses the same expansion for products with up to four factors.

<a id="pm-h-9"></a>
#### An exact identity and its consequence

The auxiliary expression below is chosen so that orthogonality yields a sum of nonnegative terms equal to zero. The resulting vanishing products will force the dichotomy.

Retain $\alpha=(1,1,1,-1,-1,-1)$ and put $$\begin{aligned}
  w&=(-3,-2,0,1,2,2),\\
  a_1&=(-1,-1,0,0,1,1),&
  a_2&=(-2,0,0,0,1,1),\\
  a_3&=(-1,-1,0,1,0,1),&
  a_4&=(-1,-1,0,1,1,0).
\end{aligned}$$ Define <a id="eq:pair-L"></a>$$L_H=-g_H(w)+\sum_{j=1}^4 c_j g_H(a_j)g_H(w-a_j),
  \qquad (c_1,c_2,c_3,c_4)=(2,1,1,1),
  \label{eq:pair-L}$$ and the two charges $$q_{\mathrm{cross}}=(2,0,0,-2,0,0),
  \qquad q_{\mathrm{same}}=(2,-2,0,0,0,0).$$ Every term defining $L_H$ has total charge $w$. The nonzero entries of $q_{\mathrm{cross}}$ lie in opposite sign triples of $\alpha$, whereas those of $q_{\mathrm{same}}$ lie in the same triple. These two choices will supply the squared-row inner products in the proof of the dichotomy.

<a id="stmt-prop-pair-certificate"></a>
<div id="prop:pair-certificate" class="proposition">

<a id="eq:pair-certificate"></a>
**Proposition 6** (Single-matrix certificate). *For every $6\times6$ Hadamard matrix $H$ and for each $q\in\{q_{\mathrm{cross}},q_{\mathrm{same}}\}$, $$3\bigl\langle |L_{\widetilde H}|^2\bigr\rangle_H
  +\left\langle
       |g_{\widetilde H}(\alpha)g_{\widetilde H}(q)|^2
    \right\rangle_H=0.
  \label{eq:pair-certificate}$$*

</div>

<details id="proof-stmt-prop-pair-certificate"><summary>Proof of stmt-prop-pair-certificate</summary>

*Proof.* Expand the two absolute squares and apply Lemma <a href="#lem:partitions" data-reference-type="ref" data-reference="lem:partitions">5</a> to each resulting product. Every product has total charge zero, at most four factors, and sum of the orders of its charges equal to ten. Consequently the resulting expression is a rational linear combination of injective moments. Apply Lemma <a href="#lem:contraction" data-reference-type="ref" data-reference="lem:contraction">4</a> until all remaining nonzero row and column orders are at least two, and choose representatives under row and column permutations, negation, and transposition.

The relations used to reduce this expression further are <a href="#eq:seven-row-contraction" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="eq:seven-row-contraction">[eq:seven-row-contraction]</a>, generated from exponent matrices with seven rows, two through four columns, positive row orders, column orders at least two, and total order at most eleven, with a chosen row of order one. The total order of an exponent matrix is the sum of its row orders, equivalently the sum of its column orders. The right sides of these relations are reduced by the same contraction procedure. Thus all relations used are consequences of Lemma <a href="#lem:contraction" data-reference-type="ref" data-reference="lem:contraction">4</a> and the stated symmetries.

Section <a href="#sec:verification" data-reference-type="ref" data-reference="sec:verification">7</a> verifies in exact rational arithmetic that substitution of these relations makes every coefficient of the expanded left side of <a href="#eq:pair-certificate" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="eq:pair-certificate">[eq:pair-certificate]</a> zero, for each of the two specified charges $q$. In particular, its elimination rules hold for every moment vector satisfying those relations, whether or not there are further relations between the moments. The complete finite computation, including the two coefficient comparisons, is included in Appendix <a href="#app:code" data-reference-type="ref" data-reference="app:code">8</a>. The resulting identity therefore holds for the moment vector of every Hadamard matrix $H$, which proves the proposition. ◻

</details>

<details id="proof-stmt-prop-pair-certificate-2"><summary>Proof of stmt-prop-pair-certificate</summary>

*Proof of Corollary <a href="#cor:dichotomy" data-reference-type="ref" data-reference="cor:dichotomy">3</a>.* The average in <a href="#eq:pair-certificate" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="eq:pair-certificate">[eq:pair-certificate]</a> is finite, and each choice of orientation and row permutation has positive weight. Every summand is nonnegative. Proposition <a href="#prop:pair-certificate" data-reference-type="ref" data-reference="prop:pair-certificate">6</a> therefore implies, separately for every ordering of the rows of $H$, $$g_{H^\pi}(\alpha)g_{H^\pi}(q_{\mathrm{cross}})=0,
  \qquad
  g_{H^\pi}(\alpha)g_{H^\pi}(q_{\mathrm{same}})=0.$$ Suppose (M) fails. The ordering witnessing its failure divides the row indices into two triples $I_+$ and $I_-$ on which the charge is $+1$ and $-1$, respectively. Permutations within either triple do not change this nonzero character. By placing any chosen $i\in I_+$ and $j\in I_-$ in positions $0$ and $3$, the first product identity gives $g_H(2(e_i-e_j))=0$. By placing any two distinct indices of $I_+$ in positions $0$ and $1$, the second product identity gives the same vanishing for that pair. Interchanging the two triples conjugates the nonzero character, since $g_H(-a)=\overline{g_H(a)}$. The same argument therefore treats pairs within $I_-$ and all remaining ordered cross pairs. Hence $$g_H\bigl(2(e_i-e_j)\bigr)=0
  \qquad\text{for every }i\ne j.$$ For distinct rows $i,j$, this says $$\sum_{k=0}^5 H(i,k)^2\overline{H(j,k)^2}
      =6g_H\bigl(2(e_i-e_j)\bigr)=0.$$ Every row of $H^{\circ2}$ has squared norm six. Thus $H^{\circ2}(H^{\circ2})^*=6I$, which for a square matrix also implies $(H^{\circ2})^*H^{\circ2}=6I$. Its entries are unimodular, so (C) holds. ◻

</details>

<a id="sec:cubic"></a>
### The cubic alternative

We now classify the second alternative of Corollary <a href="#cor:dichotomy" data-reference-type="ref" data-reference="cor:dichotomy">3</a> and complete the proof of Theorem <a href="#thm:fourier" data-reference-type="ref" data-reference="thm:fourier">1</a>. Throughout this section, put $\omega=\exp(2\pi i/3)$. Matrices satisfying the two Hadamard conditions are called S-Hadamard matrices (Lisoněk 2019, Definition 2.1) <a id="pm-cite-25"></a>[[Lisonek2019](#ref-Lisonek2019)]. The following proposition proves the order-six classification conjectured by Phangara (Phangara 2026, Conjecture 3.1.6) <a id="pm-cite-26"></a>[[Phangara2026](#ref-Phangara2026)]. Its representative is the matrix in Tao’s construction (Tao 2004, 254) <a id="pm-cite-27"></a>[[Tao2004](#ref-Tao2004)].

<a id="stmt-prop-cubic-classification"></a>
<div id="prop:cubic-classification" class="proposition">

**Proposition 7** (Classification of the cubic alternative). *Suppose that $H$ and its entrywise square $H^{\circ2}$ are Hadamard matrices of order six. By multiplying rows and columns by unit complex numbers and permuting rows and columns, one can transform $H$ into the matrix $T$ in <a href="#eq:cubic-matrix" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="eq:cubic-matrix">[eq:cubic-matrix]</a>. In particular, every entry of the transformed matrix is a cube root of unity.*

</div>

<details id="proof-stmt-prop-cubic-classification"><summary>Proof of stmt-prop-cubic-classification</summary>

*Proof.* Let $v=(v_0,\ldots,v_5)$ be the entrywise ratio of two distinct rows of $H$, and set $p_k(v)=\sum_i v_i^k$. The two Hadamard conditions give $p_1(v)=p_2(v)=0$. If $e_k$ denotes the $k$th elementary symmetric function of these six coordinates, Newton’s identities give $e_1=e_2=0$. Since $|v_i|=1$, $$e_{6-k}=e_6\overline{e_k},$$ so $e_4=e_5=0$ as well. Consequently $$\prod_{i=0}^5(z-v_i)=z^6-e_3z^3+e_6.$$ Factoring this as a quadratic in $z^3$ shows that the multiset of coordinates of $v$ is the union of two rotated cube-root triples. The triples may coincide. Thus the cubed coordinates have either one value six times or two distinct values three times each. The same conclusion holds for ratios of columns, because both matrices also have orthogonal columns.

Dephase $H$, making its initial row and column equal to one. This preserves both Hadamard conditions. Every row of $H^{\circ3}$ is now either all ones or has the value $1$ three times and a value $x\ne1$ three times. For a nonconstant row, call the three positions carrying $x$ its support; its support excludes column zero.

All nonconstant rows have the same support. Indeed, suppose two distinct supports $S,U$ have respective values $x,y\ne1$, and put $k=|S\cap U|$. As $S,U$ are three-element subsets of five positions, $k\in\{1,2\}$. The cubed row ratio has the following values and multiplicities: $$\begin{array}{c|cccc}
\text{value}&1&x&y^{-1}&xy^{-1}\\
\text{multiplicity}&k&3-k&3-k&k.
\end{array}$$ Every multiplicity is positive, whereas this ratio can have at most two distinct cubed values. The three values $1,x,y^{-1}$ therefore force $x=y^{-1}$. Next $xy^{-1}=x^2$ must equal $1$ or $x$, which forces $x=y=-1$. The two multiplicities would then be $2k$ and $6-2k$, namely $2$ and $4$ in some order, contradicting the required multiplicities $3$ and $3$. Applying the same argument to columns proves the corresponding assertion for their supports.

<a id="eq:cubic-block"></a>
Suppose that $H^{\circ3}$ is not all ones. Its common nonconstant-row support consists of three columns. Each of those columns has exactly three non-one entries, so exactly three rows are nonconstant. Comparing their values in any one of these columns shows that their non-one values coincide. After arranging these two classes of rows and columns, the cubed matrix therefore has the form $$\label{eq:cubic-block}
 H^{\circ3}=\begin{pmatrix}
 J_3&J_3\\ J_3&xJ_3
 \end{pmatrix},\qquad x\ne1,$$ where $J_3$ is the all-ones matrix of order three.

Restrict each row of $H$ to the three columns of the $xJ_3$ block. For rows from opposite classes, the ratio cubes to $x$ or $x^{-1}$ on these columns and to $1$ on the other three. The two rotated triples in that ratio are therefore separated by this division of the columns. Their sums are zero, so the restrictions of rows from opposite classes are mutually orthogonal.

Each class spans a space of dimension at least two on these three columns. To see this, suppose that the three restrictions in one class span a line. Each has squared norm three, so the inner product of any two has modulus three. Orthogonality of the full rows forces their complementary restrictions to have inner product of modulus three as well. Equality in Cauchy–Schwarz makes these complementary restrictions proportional. The three full rows would then lie in a space of dimension at most two, contrary to their orthogonality. Thus the two mutually orthogonal restricted spans have dimensions at least two, which is impossible in $\mathbb C^3$. This excludes <a href="#eq:cubic-block" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="eq:cubic-block">[eq:cubic-block]</a>. Every entry of the dephased $H$ is consequently a cube root of unity.

In each noninitial row and column, orthogonality to the initial one implies that each cube root occurs twice. There is therefore exactly one additional entry equal to one in each noninitial row and column. These entries form a matching and may be placed on the diagonal by a column permutation. Write the other entries as $\omega^{s_{ij}}$, where $s_{ij}\in\{-1,1\}$ and $i,j\in\{1,\ldots,5\}$ are distinct. Each such row has two plus signs and two minus signs.

For two distinct noninitial rows $i,j$, their ratio contains each root twice. They agree at column zero, disagree at columns $i,j$, and hence agree at exactly one of the remaining three columns. There are thus two sign mismatches among those three columns, giving $$\prod_{k\notin\{0,i,j\}}s_{ik}s_{jk}=1.$$ The product of the four signs in each complete noninitial row is also one. It follows that $s_{ij}s_{ji}=1$, so $s_{ij}=s_{ji}$. The plus signs define a simple undirected graph of degree two on five vertices. Its components are cycles of length at least three; therefore it is a single five-cycle. A simultaneous permutation of the noninitial rows and columns now gives <a href="#eq:cubic-matrix" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="eq:cubic-matrix">[eq:cubic-matrix]</a>; Figure <a href="#fig:cubic-cycle" data-reference-type="ref" data-reference="fig:cubic-cycle">1</a> shows its two off-diagonal exponent classes. For completeness, the displayed $T$ is itself Hadamard: every difference of distinct exponent rows contains each residue modulo three twice, as seen by separating an initial-row pair, an adjacent pair on the cycle, and a nonadjacent pair. Its entrywise square is $\overline T$ and is Hadamard as well. ◻

</details>

<figure id="fig:cubic-cycle">

<figcaption>The five-cycle determining the noninitial off-diagonal entries of the cubic matrix $T$. Solid edges contribute $ω$ and dashed edges contribute $ω^{−1}$. The diagonal and the initial row and column consist of ones.</figcaption>
</figure>

<a id="pm-figsrc-fig-cubic-cycle"></a>
**Figure source and visual witness** (`fig:cubic-cycle`)

TikZ/PGF source, copied verbatim from the source:

```latex
\begin{tikzpicture}[scale=1.05,every node/.style={font=\small}]
  \foreach \i/\angle in {1/90,2/18,3/-54,4/-126,5/162}
    \node[circle,draw,fill=white,inner sep=3pt] (v\i) at (\angle:1.25) {$\i$};
  \foreach \i/\j in {1/3,1/4,2/4,2/5,3/5}
    \draw[dashed,gray] (v\i)--(v\j);
  \foreach \i/\j in {1/2,2/3,3/4,4/5,5/1}
    \draw[thick,MidnightBlue] (v\i)--(v\j);
  \draw[thick,MidnightBlue] (2,-.2)--(2.5,-.2)
    node[right,black] {$t_{ij}=1$};
  \draw[dashed,gray] (2,-.7)--(2.5,-.7)
    node[right,black] {$t_{ij}=-1$};
\end{tikzpicture}
```

Visual witness description of PDF page 9 (generated from the PDF page, not source text):

```text
A graph on five numbered vertices in circles labeled 1, 2, 3, 4, 5 arranged in a regular pentagon (vertex 1 at top, 2 at top-right, 3 at bottom-right, 4 at bottom-left, 5 at top-left). The perimeter edges forming the 5-cycle (1,2), (2,3), (3,4), (4,5), and (5,1) are drawn as solid lines. The five chord edges (1,3), (1,4), (2,4), (2,5), and (3,5) forming an inner five-pointed star are drawn as dashed lines. To the right of the graph is a legend showing a solid line segment labeled $t_{ij} = 1$ and a dashed line segment labeled $t_{ij} = -1$.
```


The classification also settles the Fourier-vanishing conjecture of Matolcsi, Ruzsa, and Weiner (Matolcsi, Ruzsa, and Weiner 2013, Conjecture 2.3) <a id="pm-cite-28"></a>[[MatolcsiRuzsaWeiner2013](#ref-MatolcsiRuzsaWeiner2013)]. Here equivalence means multiplication of rows and columns by unit complex numbers and permutation of rows and columns. The matrix $T$ is equivalent to the spectral matrix $S_6$ displayed in (Tadej and Życzkowski 2006, (93)–(94)) <a id="pm-cite-29"></a>[[TadejZyczkowski2006](#ref-TadejZyczkowski2006)]: with our zero-based indices, $T_{ij}=(S_6)_{p(i),p(j)}$ for $(p(0),\ldots,p(5))=(0,1,2,5,4,3)$.

<details id="proof-stmt-prop-cubic-classification-2"><summary>Proof of stmt-prop-cubic-classification</summary>

*Proof of Theorem <a href="#thm:fourier" data-reference-type="ref" data-reference="thm:fourier">1</a>.* If $g_H(\pi\alpha)$ were nonzero for some coordinate permutation $\pi$, Corollary <a href="#cor:dichotomy" data-reference-type="ref" data-reference="cor:dichotomy">3</a> would imply that $H^{\circ2}$ is Hadamard. Proposition <a href="#prop:cubic-classification" data-reference-type="ref" data-reference="prop:cubic-classification">7</a> would then make $H$ equivalent to $T$, a contradiction. ◻

</details>

<a id="sec:complete-family"></a>
### Complete families and the cubic obstruction

We turn to Theorem <a href="#thm:main" data-reference-type="ref" data-reference="thm:main">2</a>. The first step is to exclude the cubic alternative from a complete family. We begin with the character identities that record mutual unbiasedness and completeness.

<a id="pm-h-12"></a>
#### Character identities of a complete family

Suppose that $B_1,\ldots,B_7$ are mutually unbiased orthonormal bases. For $r\ne s$, define their transition matrix by $$H_{rs}(i,k)=\sqrt6\,\langle b_{r,i},b_{s,k}\rangle.$$ Unbiasedness and orthonormality imply that $H_{rs}$ is Hadamard and that $H_{sr}=H_{rs}^*$. With a reference basis $r$ fixed, we abbreviate $H_{rs}$ and $g_{H_{rs}}$ to $H_s$ and $g_s$.

<a id="stmt-lem-basic"></a>
<div id="lem:basic" class="lemma">

<a id="eq:order-two"></a>
<a id="eq:same-matrix"></a>
<a id="eq:different-matrices"></a>
**Lemma 8**. *For each fixed reference basis the following identities hold. First, $$\label{eq:order-two}
 g_s(d)=0\quad(d\in D),
 \qquad
 \sum_{s\ne r}g_s(a)=0\quad(\mathop{\mathrm{ord}}(a)=2).$$ For arbitrary charges $a,b$ and each coordinate $i$, $$\label{eq:same-matrix}
 \sum_{j=0}^5 g_s(a+e_i-e_j)g_s(b-e_i+e_j)=g_s(a+b).$$ The same identity holds with both shifts reversed. For distinct external bases $s,t\ne r$, $$\label{eq:different-matrices}
 \sum_{d\in D}g_s(a+d)g_t(b-d)=0.$$*

</div>

The complete-set projector identity used in the proof is the second-moment identity for a complex projective $2$-design; see Zauner (Zauner 1999, Satz 2.19) <a id="pm-cite-30"></a>[[Zauner1999](#ref-Zauner1999)] and Klappenecker–Rötteler (Klappenecker and Rötteler 2005, Theorem 3) <a id="pm-cite-31"></a>[[KlappeneckerRotteler2005](#ref-KlappeneckerRotteler2005)]. We give the projection argument in our normalization. The character identities are part of the Fourier approach (Matolcsi 2012; Matolcsi, Ruzsa, and Weiner 2013) <a id="pm-cite-32"></a>[[Matolcsi2012](#ref-Matolcsi2012), [MatolcsiRuzsaWeiner2013](#ref-MatolcsiRuzsaWeiner2013)].

<details id="proof-stmt-lem-basic"><summary>Proof of stmt-lem-basic</summary>

<a id="eq:projector-identity"></a>
*Proof.* The first assertion in <a href="#eq:order-two" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="eq:order-two">[eq:order-two]</a> is row orthogonality. For the second, let $P_{s,k}=b_{s,k}b_{s,k}^*$, and let $\mathcal D_s^0$ be the complex space of traceless matrices diagonal in $B_s$. If $s\ne t$, then $$\mathop{\mathrm{Tr}}\bigl((P_{s,k}-I/6)(P_{t,l}-I/6)\bigr)=0.$$ The spaces $\mathcal D_s^0$ are therefore pairwise orthogonal for the Hilbert–Schmidt inner product. Each has dimension five, and their dimensions sum to $35=6^2-1$, the dimension of the traceless matrix space. Orthogonal projection consequently gives $$\label{eq:projector-identity}
 \mathop{\mathrm{Tr}}(XY)=\sum_{s=1}^7\sum_{k=0}^5
       \mathop{\mathrm{Tr}}(XP_{s,k})\mathop{\mathrm{Tr}}(YP_{s,k})
