 \qquad(\mathop{\mathrm{Tr}}X=\mathop{\mathrm{Tr}}Y=0).$$ This is a complex bilinear identity: it follows by pairing the complex-linear projection formula for $X$ with $Y$.

Any order-two charge is a sum $d_1+d_2$ of two members of $D$. For $s\ne r$, write $h_k$ for the $k$th column of $H_s$. In basis $B_r$, choose off-diagonal matrix units $X,Y$ whose traces against $P_{s,k}$, for $s\ne r$, are respectively $h_k^{d_1}/6$ and $h_k^{d_2}/6$. For example, $d_1=e_i-e_j$ corresponds to $X=E_{ji}$. Since $d_1+d_2$ has order two, $d_2\ne-d_1$, so $\mathop{\mathrm{Tr}}(XY)=0$. The reference basis contributes zero to <a href="#eq:projector-identity" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="eq:projector-identity">[eq:projector-identity]</a>, and each external basis contributes $g_s(d_1+d_2)/6$. This proves the second identity in <a href="#eq:order-two" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="eq:order-two">[eq:order-two]</a>.

For <a href="#eq:same-matrix" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="eq:same-matrix">[eq:same-matrix]</a>, expand the two character sums and write $x,y$ for their sampled columns. The left-hand side is $$\frac1{36}\sum_{x,y}x^ay^b\frac{x_i}{y_i}
                 \sum_{j=0}^5\frac{y_j}{x_j}.$$ The inner sum is $6$ when $x=y$ and zero otherwise, by column orthogonality. The result is $g_s(a+b)$. Conjugating the inner orthogonality relation proves the version with reversed shifts.

Finally, if $x$ is a column of $H_s$ and $y$ a column of $H_t$, their inner-product squared modulus is six. Hence $$\sum_{d\in D}(x/y)^d
 =\left|\sum_{i=0}^5\frac{x_i}{y_i}\right|^2-6=0.$$ Expanding the products of $g_s$ and $g_t$ proves <a href="#eq:different-matrices" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="eq:different-matrices">[eq:different-matrices]</a>. ◻

</details>

The identities for one matrix hold for every Hadamard matrix. The order-two sum in <a href="#eq:order-two" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="eq:order-two">[eq:order-two]</a> uses the completeness of the seven-basis set. The later mixed identities retain this completeness information through averaging over the six external bases.

<a id="pm-h-13"></a>
#### The cubic-pair obstruction

Brierley and Weigert proved the stronger fact that the standard basis and the basis defined by $T/\sqrt6$ cannot be extended by a third mutually unbiased basis (Brierley and Weigert 2009, sec. 4.1 and Table 1) <a id="pm-cite-33"></a>[[BrierleyWeigert2009Constructing](#ref-BrierleyWeigert2009Constructing)]. The following proposition gives an exact Fourier derivation of the complete-family exclusion needed here.

<a id="stmt-prop-cubic-exclusion"></a>
<div id="prop:cubic-exclusion" class="proposition">

**Proposition 9**. *In a set of seven mutually unbiased bases in $\mathbb C^6$, no transition Hadamard matrix $H$ can have $H^{\circ2}$ Hadamard.*

</div>

<a id="eq:cubic-order-two"></a>
<a id="eq:cubic-cross"></a>
<a id="eq:cubic-S"></a>
To prove the proposition, suppose that such a pair occurs. By Proposition <a href="#prop:cubic-classification" data-reference-type="ref" data-reference="prop:cubic-classification">7</a>, rephasing and reordering its basis vectors allows us to take its transition matrix to be $T$. These operations preserve every mutual unbiasedness condition. Use the first basis of this pair as reference, and call the other five transition matrices $K_1,\ldots,K_5$. Define $$f(b)=\frac15\sum_{s=1}^5g_{K_s}(b),\qquad G(b)=g_T(b).$$ Lemma <a href="#lem:basic" data-reference-type="ref" data-reference="lem:basic">8</a> gives $f(b)=G(b)=0$ at order one, and Equation <a href="#eq:order-two" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="eq:order-two">[eq:order-two]</a> gives $$\label{eq:cubic-order-two}
 f(b)=-\frac15G(b)\qquad(\mathop{\mathrm{ord}}(b)=2).$$ Equation <a href="#eq:different-matrices" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="eq:different-matrices">[eq:different-matrices]</a>, applied with its second charge equal to $-c$ and then averaged over $K_1,\ldots,K_5$, gives $$\label{eq:cubic-cross}
 \sum_{d\in D}f(a+d)\overline{G(c+d)}=0
 \qquad\text{for all balanced }a,c.$$ Let $$\label{eq:cubic-S}
 S(b)=\frac1{6!}\sum_{\pi\in S_6}
       \mathop{\mathrm{Re}}\bigl(f(\pi b)\overline{G(\pi b)}\bigr).$$ Because $f(-b)=\overline{f(b)}$ and likewise for $G$, this average also equals the average over the distinct elements of the signed permutation orbit of $b$.

<a id="eq:cubic-positivity"></a>
Every entry of $T$ has cube one, so $G(3d)=1$ for $d\in D$. The signed permutation orbit of $3(e_5-e_0)$ is precisely $\{3d:d\in D\}$. If $z$ is a uniformly sampled column of a uniformly sampled matrix among $K_1,\ldots,K_5$, then $\mathbb Ez^b=f(b)$. Consequently $$\label{eq:cubic-positivity}
 0\le\mathbb E\left|\sum_{i=0}^5z_i^3\right|^2
 =6+\sum_{d\in D}f(3d)
 =6+30S\bigl(3(e_5-e_0)\bigr).$$ Thus this orbit average must be at least $-1/5$. We will use <a href="#eq:cubic-cross" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="eq:cubic-cross">[eq:cubic-cross]</a> to compute it and contradict this lower bound. The next lemma concerns $T$ alone; it lets us calculate the coefficients of those identities one permutation orbit at a time.

<a id="pm-h-14"></a>
#### Symmetries of the cubic matrix

The row-permutation symmetry below also follows from the semilinear automorphism group determined by Gillespie, Ó Catháin, and Praeger (Gillespie, Ó Catháin, and Praeger 2018, Proposition 2) <a id="pm-cite-34"></a>[[GillespieOCathainPraeger2018](#ref-GillespieOCathainPraeger2018)]. We give a direct proof using the five-cycle classification.

<a id="stmt-lem-cubic-symmetry"></a>
<div id="lem:cubic-symmetry" class="lemma">

**Lemma 10**. *Every permutation of the rows of $T$ can be completed to a symmetry of $T$ by column permutations, row and column phase factors, and, if necessary, complex conjugation. Consequently $|G(b)|$ is constant on each orbit of balanced charges under permutations and negation.*

</div>

<details id="proof-stmt-lem-cubic-symmetry"><summary>Proof of stmt-lem-cubic-symmetry</summary>

*Proof.* A symmetry operation consists of row and column permutations, a choice of whether to conjugate, and row and column phase factors that send $T$ to itself. Normalize the phase factor of output row zero to one. The all-one output row and column then determine all the other phase factors, removing the freedom to multiply the row factors by a common phase and the column factors by its inverse.

Choose an initial row and column in $36$ ways, choose whether to conjugate, and dephase at the chosen row and column. Proposition <a href="#prop:cubic-classification" data-reference-type="ref" data-reference="prop:cubic-classification">7</a> applies again. After matching the additional ones on the diagonal, there are exactly $10$ orders of the remaining rows that, together with the matching column orders, make the plus graph the specified five-cycle: choose its initial vertex and its direction. This constructs $36\cdot2\cdot10=720$ symmetry operations. Their row and column permutations recover the chosen initial row and column as the source indices sent to position zero; together with the conjugation choice, their remaining orders recover the chosen cycle isomorphism. Thus these are distinct normalized operations.

We show that their induced row permutations are distinct. Work with exponents modulo three, and write $t_j=(t_{ij})_{i=0}^5$ for column $j$ of the exponent matrix. A symmetry fixing the row order acts on the set $X=\{t_0,\ldots,t_5\}$ as $$X\longmapsto sX+u,\qquad s\in\{1,-1\}.$$ Indeed, because row zero has only zero exponents, the column phase factors are common and may be absorbed into the row factors. The remaining row factors have exponents modulo three, and the image of the zero column shows that $u\in X$.

For $j\ne0$, column $t_j$ has exactly one zero in its noninitial positions, at position $j$. Hence $-t_j\notin X$: its zero would force it to equal $t_j$, which it does not. A nonzero translation preserving $X$ would contain both $u$ and $2u=-u$, and is therefore impossible. Negation with $u=0$ is impossible for the same reason.

Finally suppose $s=-1$ and $u=t_x\ne0$. Choose a neighbor $y$ of $x$ on the five-cycle, and let $z$ be their common non-neighbor. Directly from <a href="#eq:cubic-matrix" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="eq:cubic-matrix">[eq:cubic-matrix]</a>, $t_x-t_y$ has its unique noninitial zero at position $z$: at the other two positions outside $\{x,y,z\}$, exactly one of $x,y$ is adjacent to that vertex, so the differences are $\pm2$, hence nonzero modulo three. Were $t_x-t_y$ a column, it would have to be $t_z$. But at positions $x,y$ the former has entries $-1,1$, while $t_z$ has entries $-1,-1$, a contradiction modulo three. Thus the only symmetry fixing the row order is the identity, after the irrelevant common phase normalization. Two of the $720$ operations with the same row permutation would have such a quotient, so their row permutations are all distinct. They exhaust the $720$ possible row permutations.

For a balanced charge, column phases cancel in the defining character, while row phases multiply it by a unit complex number. Conjugation conjugates the character. These observations prove the last assertion, including negation since $G(-b)=\overline{G(b)}$. ◻

</details>

<a id="pm-h-15"></a>
#### Coefficients of the cross identities

<a id="eq:cubic-R"></a>
<a id="eq:cubic-weighted-cross"></a>
Let $$a=(-1,-1,0,0,1,1),\qquad c=(0,0,0,0,-1,1),$$ and let $\mathcal O$ be the set of distinct simultaneous permutations $(A,C)$ of $(a,c)$. Apply <a href="#eq:cubic-cross" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="eq:cubic-cross">[eq:cubic-cross]</a> to every $(A,C)\in\mathcal O$, multiply by $\overline{G(A-C)}$, sum, and take real parts. To record the coefficients explicitly, set $$Q(b)=\sum_{\substack{(A,C)\in\mathcal O\\b-A\in D}}
                 G(A-C)G(b-A+C).$$ The resulting equality is $$\sum_b\mathop{\mathrm{Re}}\bigl(f(b)\overline{Q(b)}\bigr)=0,$$ where the sum is finite. For a complex number $z$, write $z^{[1]}=z$ and $z^{[-1]}=\overline z$. Conjugacy of $f$ permits symmetrizing the coefficient as $R(b)=(Q(b)+\overline{Q(-b)})/2$: replacing $b$ by $-b$ in the finite sum above and averaging leaves it unchanged. Explicitly, $$\label{eq:cubic-R}
 R(b)=\frac12
 \sum_{\substack{(A,C)\in\mathcal O,\ \epsilon\in\{-1,1\}\\
                  \epsilon b-A\in D}}
 \bigl[G(A-C)G(\epsilon b-A+C)\bigr]^{[\epsilon]}.$$ We have therefore obtained the weighted cross identity $$\label{eq:cubic-weighted-cross}
 \sum_b\mathop{\mathrm{Re}}\bigl(f(b)\overline{R(b)}\bigr)=0.$$ Its coefficients depend only on $T$. We now calculate them.

<a id="eq:cubic-count-interpretation"></a>
The following table lists representatives $w_j$ of every balanced charge of order at most three, up to permutation and negation. The integer triples in its last two columns satisfy $$\label{eq:cubic-count-interpretation}
 6G(w_j)=\sum_{m=0}^2N_m\omega^m,
 \qquad
 72R(w_j)=\sum_{m=0}^2U_m\omega^m.$$

<div id="tab:cubic-counts">

| $j$ | $w_j$              | $(N_0,N_1,N_2)$ | $(U_0,U_1,U_2)$ |
|----:|:-------------------|:---------------:|:---------------:|
|   0 | $(-3,0,0,0,0,3)$   |    $(6,0,0)$    |    $(0,0,0)$    |
|   1 | $(-3,0,0,0,1,2)$   |    $(2,2,2)$    |    $(0,0,0)$    |
|   2 | $(-3,0,0,1,1,1)$   |    $(3,0,3)$    |    $(0,0,0)$    |
|   3 | $(-2,-1,0,0,1,2)$  |    $(4,1,1)$    |  $(60,42,42)$   |
|   4 | $(-2,-1,0,1,1,1)$  |    $(2,2,2)$    | $(144,144,144)$ |
|   5 | $(-2,0,0,0,0,2)$   |    $(2,2,2)$    |    $(0,0,0)$    |
|   6 | $(-2,0,0,0,1,1)$   |    $(3,3,0)$    | $(180,180,72)$  |
|   7 | $(-1,-1,-1,1,1,1)$ |    $(3,3,0)$    | $(540,540,216)$ |
|   8 | $(-1,-1,0,0,1,1)$  |    $(4,1,1)$    | $(480,336,336)$ |
|   9 | $(-1,0,0,0,0,1)$   |    $(2,2,2)$    | $(576,576,576)$ |
|  10 | $(0,0,0,0,0,0)$    |    $(6,0,0)$    |    $(0,0,0)$    |

Exact cube-root counts for $G$ and $R$.

</div>

Here is an explicit summation rule reproducing Table <a href="#tab:cubic-counts" data-reference-type="ref" data-reference="tab:cubic-counts">1</a>. Put $$v(b,k)=\sum_{i=0}^5 b_i t_{ik}\pmod3,$$ with residues in $\{0,1,2\}$. For each input $b=w_j$, initialize two separate arrays $N,U$ of three zero integers and apply these loops:

>     for k = 0,...,5:
>         N[v(b,k)] += 1
>     for p,q = 0,...,5 with p != q:
>         for 0 <= h < i <= 5 with {h,i} disjoint from {p,q}:
>             A = e_p + e_q - e_h - e_i
>             u = 2*e_p - e_h - e_i
>             for s in {-1,1} with s*b - A in D:
>                 for k,l = 0,...,5:
>                     U[(s*(v(u,k) + v(s*b-u,l))) mod 3] += 1

Indeed, the $30\binom42=180$ pairs in $\mathcal O$ are uniquely parametrized by $$A=e_p+e_q-e_h-e_i,\qquad C=e_q-e_p,$$ with precisely the conditions used in the loops; thus $u=A-C$. Each product of two $G$ terms contributes $36$ cube roots with coefficient $1/36$, and the factor $1/2$ in <a href="#eq:cubic-R" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="eq:cubic-R">[eq:cubic-R]</a> accounts for the denominator $72$ in <a href="#eq:cubic-count-interpretation" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="eq:cubic-count-interpretation">[eq:cubic-count-interpretation]</a>.

<a id="eq:cubic-R-multipliers"></a>
For example, row $3$ gives $G(w_3)=(4+\omega+\omega^2)/6=1/2$ and $R(w_3)=(60+42\omega+42\omega^2)/72=1/4$. Using $1+\omega+\omega^2=0$ in every row gives $$\label{eq:cubic-R-multipliers}
 R(b)=\lambda_jG(b),\qquad
 \lambda_3=\tfrac12,\quad \lambda_6=3,\quad
 \lambda_7=9,\quad \lambda_8=4,$$ on the signed permutation orbit of $w_j$, and $R(b)=0$ on all other orbits. To justify extending the representative calculation, observe that every factor in <a href="#eq:cubic-R" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="eq:cubic-R">[eq:cubic-R]</a> has a balanced charge. Its column phases therefore cancel. The two charges in each product add to $\epsilon b$, so after the prescribed conjugation its row phase factor is exactly the factor for $G(b)$. Row permutations preserve the full set $\mathcal O$, and conjugation conjugates both $R$ and $G$. Lemma <a href="#lem:cubic-symmetry" data-reference-type="ref" data-reference="lem:cubic-symmetry">10</a> therefore gives the asserted covariance on every orbit; also $R(-b)=\overline{R(b)}$. No other orders can occur in the support of $R$, since $\epsilon b=A+d$ with $\mathop{\mathrm{ord}}(A)=2$ and $d\in D$ implies $\mathop{\mathrm{ord}}(b)\le3$.

<a id="pm-h-16"></a>
#### The contradiction

<details id="proof-stmt-lem-cubic-symmetry-2"><summary>Proof of stmt-lem-cubic-symmetry</summary>

<a id="eq:cubic-first-relation"></a>
*Completion of the proof of Proposition <a href="#prop:cubic-exclusion" data-reference-type="ref" data-reference="prop:cubic-exclusion">9</a>.* Write $S_j=S(w_j)$ for the representatives in Table <a href="#tab:cubic-counts" data-reference-type="ref" data-reference="tab:cubic-counts">1</a>. The orbit sizes for $j=3,6,7,8$ are respectively $360,120,20,90$. By <a href="#eq:cubic-R-multipliers" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="eq:cubic-R-multipliers">[eq:cubic-R-multipliers]</a>, their coefficients in <a href="#eq:cubic-weighted-cross" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="eq:cubic-weighted-cross">[eq:cubic-weighted-cross]</a> are $180,360,180,360$. Dividing by $180$ proves $$\label{eq:cubic-first-relation}
 S_3+2S_6+S_7+2S_8=0.$$

<a id="eq:cubic-known-S"></a>
Table <a href="#tab:cubic-counts" data-reference-type="ref" data-reference="tab:cubic-counts">1</a> and Lemma <a href="#lem:cubic-symmetry" data-reference-type="ref" data-reference="lem:cubic-symmetry">10</a> imply $$S_1=S_4=S_5=S_9=0.$$ At types $6$ and $8$ the squared modulus of $G$ is $1/4$, so <a href="#eq:cubic-order-two" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="eq:cubic-order-two">[eq:cubic-order-two]</a> gives $$\label{eq:cubic-known-S}
 S_6=S_8=-\frac1{20}.$$

<a id="eq:cubic-last-relations"></a>
Next take $a=c=w_5$ in <a href="#eq:cubic-cross" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="eq:cubic-cross">[eq:cubic-cross]</a> and average over coordinate permutations. Do the same with $a=c=w_8$. Classifying the $30$ charges $w_j+d$, $d\in D$, by their signed permutation orbits gives the following counts: $$\begin{array}{c|rrrrrrrr}
 &0&1&3&4&6&7&8&9\\\hline
 w_5+D&1&8&12&0&8&0&0&1\\
 w_8+D&0&0&4&8&4&2&8&4.
\end{array}$$ These counts can be obtained simply by adding each of the $30$ vectors $e_i-e_j$ to the displayed representatives and sorting the coordinates, allowing a simultaneous change of sign. Averaging over permutations turns each resulting charge into its orbit average in <a href="#eq:cubic-S" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="eq:cubic-S">[eq:cubic-S]</a>. Substituting the known values gives $$\label{eq:cubic-last-relations}
 S_0+12S_3=\frac25,
 \qquad 4S_3+2S_7=\frac35.$$ Together, <a href="#eq:cubic-first-relation" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="eq:cubic-first-relation">[eq:cubic-first-relation]</a>, <a href="#eq:cubic-known-S" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="eq:cubic-known-S">[eq:cubic-known-S]</a>, and <a href="#eq:cubic-last-relations" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="eq:cubic-last-relations">[eq:cubic-last-relations]</a> yield $$S_3=S_7=\frac1{10},\qquad S_0=-\frac45.$$

Since $w_0=3(e_5-e_0)$, equation <a href="#eq:cubic-positivity" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="eq:cubic-positivity">[eq:cubic-positivity]</a> now gives $$\mathbb E\left|\sum_{i=0}^5z_i^3\right|^2
 =6+30S_0=-18,$$ contradicting nonnegativity. This proves the proposition. ◻

</details>

<a id="sec:mixed"></a>
### Mixed moments and the remaining case

<a id="eq:all-M"></a>
Suppose that a complete set of seven mutually unbiased bases exists and that its transition matrices satisfy $$\label{eq:all-M}
 g_{H_{rs}}(P\alpha)=0
 \qquad(r\ne s,\;P\text{ a coordinate permutation}).$$ We seek a nonnegative averaged quadratic form whose expansion in bounded moments has a strictly negative upper bound. Corollary <a href="#cor:dichotomy" data-reference-type="ref" data-reference="cor:dichotomy">3</a> and Proposition <a href="#prop:cubic-exclusion" data-reference-type="ref" data-reference="prop:cubic-exclusion">9</a> will then exclude a complete set.

<a id="pm-h-18"></a>
#### Sampling and positive forms

<a id="eq:mixed-moment"></a>
Choose a reference basis index $r$ uniformly from the seven indices. Given $r$, choose independently a uniform permutation $\sigma$ of its six coordinates and a uniform bijection $\pi$ from $\{0,\ldots,5\}$ to the six other basis indices. In this section write $$H_s(i,k)=\sqrt6\,\langle b_{r,\sigma(i)},b_{\pi(s),k}\rangle,
 \qquad g_s=g_{H_s}.$$ The column order within each basis may remain fixed: each $g_s$ already averages over all six columns. For labels $\mathbf s=(s_1,\ldots,s_n)$ and balanced charges $\mathbf a=(a_1,\ldots,a_n)$ with $\sum_j a_j=0$, define $$\label{eq:mixed-moment}
 \mathcal M(\mathbf s;\mathbf a)
   =\mathbb E\,\mathop{\mathrm{Re}}\!\left(\prod_{j=1}^n g_{s_j}(a_j)\right).$$ Equal labels always denote the same matrix. The marginal assignment of any $l$ distinct labels is a uniform injection into the six nonreference bases. Thus the definition does not depend on which unused labels are included in the sampled bijection. The empty product has moment $1$.

<a id="eq:average-pair-moment"></a>
<a id="eq:moment-bounds"></a>
We also use the ordered-pair average of the injective moments of Section <a href="#sec:pair" data-reference-type="ref" data-reference="sec:pair">3</a>: $$\label{eq:average-pair-moment}
 m(A)=\frac1{42}\sum_{r\ne s}m_{H_{rs}}(A),
 \qquad
 H_{rs}(i,k)=\sqrt6\,\langle b_{r,i},b_{s,k}\rangle.$$ Here $m_H$ is the real injective moment with the transpose averaging specified there. All these actual moments obey $$\label{eq:moment-bounds}
 |m(A)|\le1,\qquad |\mathcal M(\mathbf s;\mathbf a)|\le1.$$ For the first inequality the integrand has modulus one. For the second, each $g_s(a)$ is an average of six numbers of modulus one.

The next construction expresses the real parts of Gram entries as linear combinations of three-factor mixed moments. The third factor comes from one column shared by all the functions.

<a id="eq:F-functions"></a>
In addition to the preceding sampling, choose a uniform column $z$ of $H_0$. For every balanced charge $a$, define functions on this probability space by $$\label{eq:F-functions}
 F_0(a)=g_0(a)z^{-a},\qquad
 F_1(a)=\left(\frac15\sum_{s=1}^5g_s(a)\right)z^{-a}.$$ The same sampled column $z$ is used in every function $F_t(a)$. This use of moments and positive Gram forms follows the broader polynomial-optimization approach to MUBs (Brierley and Weigert 2010; Gribling and Polak 2024) <a id="pm-cite-35"></a>[[BrierleyWeigert2010SDP](#ref-BrierleyWeigert2010SDP), [GriblingPolak2024](#ref-GriblingPolak2024)]; the shared-column functions above specify the particular forms used here.

In the formulas below, an index $1$ on $F$ denotes the average over five bases in <a href="#eq:F-functions" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="eq:F-functions">[eq:F-functions]</a>; a label $1$ in $\mathcal M$ denotes a representative of those bases after taking expectation.

<a id="stmt-lem-gram"></a>
<div id="lem:gram" class="lemma">

<a id="eq:gram-second"></a>
**Lemma 11** (Gram entries). *For balanced $a,v$, put $\mathbf b=(a,-v,v-a)$. If $(s,t)\in\{0,1\}^2$ and $(s,t)\ne(1,1)$, then <a id="eq:gram-first"></a>$$\label{eq:gram-first}
 \mathop{\mathrm{Re}}\,\mathbb E\bigl(F_s(a)\overline{F_t(v)}\bigr)
       =\mathcal M((s,t,0);\mathbf b).$$ For the remaining case, $$\label{eq:gram-second}
 \mathop{\mathrm{Re}}\,\mathbb E\bigl(F_1(a)\overline{F_1(v)}\bigr)
   =\frac15\mathcal M((1,1,0);\mathbf b)
    +\frac45\mathcal M((1,2,0);\mathbf b).$$*

</div>

<details id="proof-stmt-lem-gram"><summary>Proof of stmt-lem-gram</summary>

*Proof.* Conditioning first on all the matrices and averaging over $z$ gives $\mathbb E_z z^{v-a}=g_0(v-a)$. In a term with exactly one $F_1$, the five summands have the same expectation by label exchangeability. In a term with two $F_1$ factors, expand the two sums independently. Of the $25$ ordered choices of labels from $\{1,\ldots,5\}$, five have equal labels and twenty have distinct labels. Their marginal distributions give <a href="#eq:gram-second" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="eq:gram-second">[eq:gram-second]</a>. This averaging removes the auxiliary column completely. A zero charge contributes $g_s(0)=1$ and may be deleted; removing any unused label preserves the uniform injection law already described. ◻

</details>

<a id="pm-h-19"></a>
#### Reducing the Gram entries

The three-factor moments in Lemma <a href="#lem:gram" data-reference-type="ref" data-reference="lem:gram">11</a> can be simplified using symmetry, character vanishing, and the identities of a complete family. When all factors refer to one matrix, the injective moments and equations from Section <a href="#sec:pair" data-reference-type="ref" data-reference="sec:pair">3</a> apply as well.

<a id="stmt-lem-mixed-reductions"></a>
<div id="lem:mixed-reductions" class="lemma">

**Lemma 12** (Mixed-moment reductions). *Under <a href="#eq:all-M" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="eq:all-M">[eq:all-M]</a>, the following operations and identities are valid.*

1.  *A common coordinate permutation of all charges, simultaneous negation of all charges, a simultaneous reordering of factors and their labels, and an arbitrary injective renaming of the used labels preserve <a href="#eq:mixed-moment" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="eq:mixed-moment">[eq:mixed-moment]</a>. A zero charge factor may be omitted. Coordinates on which every charge is zero may also be omitted, with the remaining coordinates interpreted as a uniform injection into the six row positions.*

2.  *The moment vanishes if any charge has order one or is a coordinate permutation of $\alpha$.*

<a id="eq:singleton-reduction"></a>
3.  *Suppose that $a_j$ has order two and its label $s_j$ occurs only once. Let $S$ be the set of distinct used labels and $l=|S|$. For each $t\in S\setminus\{s_j\}$, let $\mathbf s^{j\to t}$ be obtained from $\mathbf s$ by replacing $s_j$ with $t$. Then $$\label{eq:singleton-reduction}
     \mathcal M(\mathbf s;\mathbf a)
     =-\frac1{7-l}\sum_{t\in S\setminus\{s_j\}}
           \mathcal M(\mathbf s^{j\to t};\mathbf a).$$*

<a id="eq:single-label-partitions"></a>
4.  *If all labels are equal, the moment is a linear combination of the pair moments <a href="#eq:average-pair-moment" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="eq:average-pair-moment">[eq:average-pair-moment]</a>. More precisely, let $A$ have columns $a_1,\ldots,a_n$, after removing its zero rows. For a set partition $\mathcal P$ of $\{1,\ldots,n\}$, let $A_{\mathcal P}$ have one column $\sum_{j\in B}a_j$ for each block $B\in\mathcal P$. Then $$\label{eq:single-label-partitions}
     \mathcal M((s,\ldots,s);\mathbf a)
     =\sum_{\substack{\mathcal P\text{ a set partition}\\|\mathcal P|\le6}}
           \frac{(6)_{|\mathcal P|}}{6^n}\,m(A_{\mathcal P}),$$ where $(6)_k=6\cdot5\cdots(6-k+1)$. Zero rows or columns in $A_{\mathcal P}$ may be deleted as in Section <a href="#sec:pair" data-reference-type="ref" data-reference="sec:pair">3</a>.*

<a id="eq:mixed-same"></a>
<a id="eq:mixed-different"></a>
5.  *For balanced $a,b,c$ with $a+b+c=0$, $$\begin{aligned}
     &\sum_{j=0}^5
     \mathcal M\bigl((s,s,u);
           (a+e_i-e_j,b-e_i+e_j,c)\bigr)
       =\mathcal M\bigl((s,u);(a+b,c)\bigr),
           \label{eq:mixed-same}\\
     &\sum_{d\in D}
     \mathcal M\bigl((s,t,u);(a+d,b-d,c)\bigr)=0
           \qquad(s\ne t).
           \label{eq:mixed-different}
     
    \end{aligned}$$ Equation <a href="#eq:mixed-same" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="eq:mixed-same">[eq:mixed-same]</a> also holds with the signs of both shifts reversed. The third label $u$ may coincide with another label.*

</div>

<details id="proof-stmt-lem-mixed-reductions"><summary>Proof of stmt-lem-mixed-reductions</summary>

*Proof.* Uniform sampling gives the coordinate and label symmetries in (i), while commutativity gives factor reordering. Simultaneous negation conjugates the whole product, leaving its real part unchanged. A zero charge gives $g_s(0)=1$. Removing unused labels or coordinates leaves a uniform injection on those retained. Part (ii) follows from Hadamard orthogonality and <a href="#eq:all-M" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="eq:all-M">[eq:all-M]</a>. In particular, a permuted $\alpha$ has six nonzero coordinates; deleting zero coordinate rows cannot change whether a charge is of this type.

For (iii), fix $r$, the row permutation, and the bases assigned to the $l-1$ other used labels. There are $6-(l-1)=7-l$ available bases for the singleton label. For this fixed row order, let $\widetilde g_v$ denote the character average for the external basis $v$. Equation <a href="#eq:order-two" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="eq:order-two">[eq:order-two]</a> gives $$\sum_{\substack{v\ne r\\v\text{ not already assigned}}}
       \widetilde g_v(a_j)
 =-\sum_{t\in S\setminus\{s_j\}}g_t(a_j),$$ with the same row order throughout. Multiply by the other factors and average over the remaining choices. The marginal distribution of the $l-1$ retained labels is exactly the distribution defining each moment on the right of <a href="#eq:singleton-reduction" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="eq:singleton-reduction">[eq:singleton-reduction]</a>.

For (iv), expand each $g_s$ as its column average and group the resulting $n$ column choices by their equality partition. A partition with $k$ blocks has $(6)_k$ distinct assignments to its blocks, out of $6^n$ choices, giving the displayed coefficient. The retained rows are sampled injectively. It remains to justify the transpose averaging in $m(A)$. The marginal ordered pair of bases is uniform, and $H_{sr}=\overline{H_{rs}}^{\,\mathsf T}$. Exchanging $r$ and $s$ exchanges the row and column injections and sends an exponent matrix $A$ to $-A^{\mathsf T}$. Negating all exponents conjugates the monomial, leaving its real part unchanged. Thus the ordered-pair average is already invariant under transposition. This proves <a href="#eq:single-label-partitions" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="eq:single-label-partitions">[eq:single-label-partitions]</a>, including the transpose convention in <a href="#eq:average-pair-moment" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="eq:average-pair-moment">[eq:average-pair-moment]</a>.

Finally, multiply <a href="#eq:same-matrix" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="eq:same-matrix">[eq:same-matrix]</a> or <a href="#eq:different-matrices" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="eq:different-matrices">[eq:different-matrices]</a> by $g_u(c)$ and take real expectations. These pointwise identities give (v). ◻

</details>

In the finite calculation below, a mixed key consists of its factor labels and the matrix whose columns are its charges. The reductions in Lemma <a href="#lem:mixed-reductions" data-reference-type="ref" data-reference="lem:mixed-reductions">12</a> apply to these keys. Single-label keys are expanded into the moments $m(A)$, and the pair relations from Section <a href="#sec:pair" data-reference-type="ref" data-reference="sec:pair">3</a> may then be used. If a single-label expression vanishes by part (ii), its expansion also gives a valid homogeneous relation among the $m(A)$. These steps preserve the interpretation of every remaining symbol as an actual moment.

<a id="pm-h-20"></a>
#### The six positive forms

Here are the finite collections of functions and integer weights used in the certificate. The exact data are the literal list named `data` in Appendix <a href="#app:code" data-reference-type="ref" data-reference="app:code">8</a>, also supplied in the companion file `certificate/verify.py`. Each record has the form $$(\lambda,\ \texttt{ws},\ e_\lambda,\ \texttt{a}).$$ The list $\texttt{ws}=((t_0,v_0),\ldots,(t_{m-1},v_{m-1}))$ specifies $m$ pairs with $t_i\in\{0,1\}$ and balanced $v_i\in\mathbb Z^6$. The integer list $\texttt{a}$ specifies a symmetric $m\times m$ matrix $Z_\lambda$ by its lower triangle in row order: with zero-based list indices, <a id="eq:Z-encoding"></a>$$\label{eq:Z-encoding}
 (Z_\lambda)_{ij}=(Z_\lambda)_{ji}
       =\texttt{a}_{\,i(i+1)/2+j}
       \qquad(0\le j\le i<m).$$ Thus the data define the matrices and lists without any optimization or choice of numerical tolerances. Their sizes and scales are

<div class="center">

| $\lambda$   | $m$ | $e_\lambda$ |
|:------------|----:|------------:|
| $(1,1,1,2)$ |   5 |          14 |
| $(1,1,3)$   |  21 |          13 |
| $(1,2,2)$   |  11 |          13 |
| $(1,4)$     |  27 |          14 |
| $(2,3)$     |  22 |          14 |
| $(5)$       |  12 |          15 |

</div>

<a id="eq:Young-functions"></a>
For each of these six lists of row lengths $\lambda$, place the positions $1,\ldots,5$, in order, in successive left-justified rows of lengths $\lambda_1,\lambda_2,\ldots$. Let $R_\lambda$ consist of permutations within these rows, and let $C_\lambda$ consist of permutations within the resulting columns. Both groups act on $\{0,\ldots,5\}$ by fixing position $0$. Our convention for their action on charges is $$(P_pv)_i=v_{p(i)}.$$ These signed row and column permutation sums are the usual Young-symmetrizer construction. Symmetry reduction of positive Gram forms is developed generally in (Gatermann and Parrilo 2004) <a id="pm-cite-36"></a>[[GatermannParrilo2004](#ref-GatermannParrilo2004)] and for MUB moment problems in (Gribling and Polak 2024) <a id="pm-cite-37"></a>[[GriblingPolak2024](#ref-GriblingPolak2024)]. Here the finite sums are given explicitly, so their use requires no representation-theoretic decomposition theorem. Define the vector $Y_\lambda$ by $$\label{eq:Young-functions}
 (Y_\lambda)_i
    =\sum_{\substack{p\in R_\lambda\\q\in C_\lambda}}
          \operatorname{sgn}(q)\,
          F_{t_i}(P_pP_qv_i)
       \qquad(0\le i<m).$$ In particular, $(P_pP_qv)_i=v_{q(p(i))}$, which is the order implemented by `perm(perm(v,q),p)` in the appendix. Formula <a href="#eq:Young-functions" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="eq:Young-functions">[eq:Young-functions]</a> defines a finite linear combination of the functions <a href="#eq:F-functions" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="eq:F-functions">[eq:F-functions]</a> for every listed pair.

<a id="stmt-prop-mixed-certificate"></a>
<div id="prop:mixed-certificate" class="proposition">

<a id="eq:mixed-certificate"></a>
**Proposition 13** (Mixed-moment certificate). *Each matrix $Z_\lambda$ specified above is positive definite. Under the mixed-moment identities of Lemma <a href="#lem:mixed-reductions" data-reference-type="ref" data-reference="lem:mixed-reductions">12</a>, the forms <a href="#eq:Young-functions" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="eq:Young-functions">[eq:Young-functions]</a> satisfy $$\begin{aligned}
 5\sum_\lambda 10^{15-e_\lambda}
       \mathbb E\bigl(Y_\lambda^*Z_\lambda Y_\lambda\bigr)
 &\le
       \frac{-613302797399911+179721388988719}{6480}
       \label{eq:mixed-certificate}\\
 &=-\frac{2007321335237}{30}<0.\notag
\end{aligned}$$*

</div>

<details id="proof-stmt-prop-mixed-certificate"><summary>Proof of stmt-prop-mixed-certificate</summary>

*Proof.* The exact finite calculations establishing the matrix and coefficient claims are detailed in Section <a href="#sec:verification" data-reference-type="ref" data-reference="sec:verification">7</a>, with their full integer and rational arithmetic in Appendix <a href="#app:code" data-reference-type="ref" data-reference="app:code">8</a>. We specify here the mathematical expansion to which those checks apply.

First expand each $Y_\lambda$ by <a href="#eq:Young-functions" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="eq:Young-functions">[eq:Young-functions]</a>, and replace every real Gram entry using Lemma <a href="#lem:gram" data-reference-type="ref" data-reference="lem:gram">11</a>. Multiplication by five makes its weights either $5$ or the pair $(1,4)$. Because $Z_\lambda$ is real symmetric, summing its diagonal contributions and twice its lower off-diagonal contributions gives the real quantity $\mathbb E(Y_\lambda^*Z_\lambda Y_\lambda)$. The factor $10^{15-e_\lambda}$ is retained for each block.

<a id="eq:mixed-residual"></a>
Apply the mixed reductions and the homogeneous relations <a href="#eq:mixed-same" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="eq:mixed-same">[eq:mixed-same]</a>–<a href="#eq:mixed-different" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="eq:mixed-different">[eq:mixed-different]</a>, using the finite selection of relations with total charge order at most $11$ described in Section <a href="#sec:verification" data-reference-type="ref" data-reference="sec:verification">7</a>. That selection only restricts which valid relations are used. The exact substitutions give $$\label{eq:mixed-residual}
 5\sum_\lambda 10^{15-e_\lambda}
       \mathbb E\bigl(Y_\lambda^*Z_\lambda Y_\lambda\bigr)
    = f+\sum_{\rho\in\mathcal R}\beta_\rho X_\rho,$$ where each $X_\rho$ is a nonconstant symbol representing one of the original moments $m(A)$ or $\mathcal M(\mathbf s;\mathbf a)$, and <a id="eq:mixed-coefficients"></a>$$\label{eq:mixed-coefficients}
 f=-\frac{613302797399911}{6480},\qquad
 \sum_{\rho\in\mathcal R}|\beta_\rho|
     =\frac{179721388988719}{6480}.$$ The linear-algebra verification in Section <a href="#sec:verification" data-reference-type="ref" data-reference="sec:verification">7</a> proves that these substitutions follow from the chosen equations. The bounds <a href="#eq:moment-bounds" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="eq:moment-bounds">[eq:moment-bounds]</a> applied to <a href="#eq:mixed-residual" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="eq:mixed-residual">[eq:mixed-residual]</a> now prove <a href="#eq:mixed-certificate" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="eq:mixed-certificate">[eq:mixed-certificate]</a>.

For each $Z_\lambda$, the exact Schur-complement calculation in Section <a href="#sec:verification" data-reference-type="ref" data-reference="sec:verification">7</a> verifies positive pivots and hence positive definiteness. ◻

</details>

<details id="proof-stmt-prop-mixed-certificate-2"><summary>Proof of stmt-prop-mixed-certificate</summary>

*Completion of the proof of Theorem <a href="#thm:main" data-reference-type="ref" data-reference="thm:main">2</a>.* If seven mutually unbiased bases existed, Corollary <a href="#cor:dichotomy" data-reference-type="ref" data-reference="cor:dichotomy">3</a> would give case (M) or case (C) for each transition matrix. Proposition <a href="#prop:cubic-exclusion" data-reference-type="ref" data-reference="prop:cubic-exclusion">9</a> excludes case (C), so all pairs satisfy <a href="#eq:all-M" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="eq:all-M">[eq:all-M]</a> and the preceding construction applies. For a complex vector $Y=x+\mathrm i y$ and a real symmetric positive definite matrix $Z$, $$Y^*ZY=x^{\mathsf T}Zx+y^{\mathsf T}Zy\ge0.$$ Consequently the left side of <a href="#eq:mixed-certificate" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="eq:mixed-certificate">[eq:mixed-certificate]</a> is nonnegative. Its strictly negative upper bound is a contradiction. This proves Theorem <a href="#thm:main" data-reference-type="ref" data-reference="thm:main">2</a>. ◻

</details>

<a id="sec:verification"></a>
### Exact verification of the two certificates

We now give the finite calculations establishing Propositions <a href="#prop:pair-certificate" data-reference-type="ref" data-reference="prop:pair-certificate">6</a> and <a href="#prop:mixed-certificate" data-reference-type="ref" data-reference="prop:mixed-certificate">13</a>. The complete program and all its data are printed in Appendix <a href="#app:code" data-reference-type="ref" data-reference="app:code">8</a>. It specifies the integer matrices, coordinate orders, and finite enumerations used below. All arithmetic that certifies an equality or inequality is integer or rational arithmetic. Modular arithmetic supplies candidate substitutions, whose validity is then checked exactly. Rational Gram certificates and exact positivity checks are established methods; see Peyrl–Parrilo (Peyrl and Parrilo 2008, sec. 3) <a id="pm-cite-38"></a>[[PeyrlParrilo2008](#ref-PeyrlParrilo2008)]. Our input matrices are specified integers, and the proof below certifies the displayed identities directly from those data.

The calculation has three steps. First, expand each expression in named moments using the identities already proved. Next, use a finite set of true linear equations to substitute for some of these moments. Finally, check either that every coefficient vanishes, for the pair certificate, or that the constant coefficient plus the absolute sum of the remaining coefficients is negative, for the mixed certificate. We first justify the substitutions for arbitrary real moment values, then specify the equations and coefficient checks for each certificate.

<a id="pm-h-22"></a>
#### Exact certification of linear substitutions

Both certificates use finite systems of linear equations in their moment coordinates. The same substitution procedure applies to either system. Include the constant moment as a coordinate, so that every moment equation is homogeneous in the enlarged coordinate vector. Clearing denominators turns the equations into integer rows. The lists called `I` in the program specify a fixed priority order on the coordinates. This choice affects the form of the answer and, in the mixed calculation, the size of its residual coefficient sum; it does not add any relation.

<a id="stmt-lem-exact-elimination"></a>
<div id="lem:exact-elimination" class="lemma">

**Lemma 14** (Exact substitutions). *Let $W\in\mathbb Z^{r\times N}$ consist of selected moment equations after their denominators have been cleared. Suppose a set $J$ of $r$ columns gives a square submatrix $W_J$ invertible modulo a prime. Put $F=\{1,\ldots,N\}\setminus J$. If a rational matrix $P\in\mathbb Q^{N\times N}$ satisfies $$P_{F,F}=I,\qquad P_{*,J}=0,\qquad WP=0,$$ then every real vector $\mathbf m$ satisfying $W\mathbf m=0$ obeys $\mathbf m=P\mathbf m$. Thus the rows of $P$ give valid substitutions for all coordinates in terms of the coordinates indexed by $F$.*

</div>

<details id="proof-stmt-lem-exact-elimination"><summary>Proof of stmt-lem-exact-elimination</summary>

*Proof.* Invertibility modulo the prime implies that the integer $\det W_J$ is nonzero. In the coordinate order $J,F$, write $$W=\begin{pmatrix}W_J&W_F\end{pmatrix},\qquad
 P=\begin{pmatrix}0&P_{J,F}\\0&I\end{pmatrix}.$$ The exact equality $WP=0$ gives $P_{J,F}=-W_J^{-1}W_F$. Every solution of $W\mathbf m=0$ consequently satisfies $\mathbf m_J=P_{J,F}\mathbf m_F$, while the free coordinates of $P\mathbf m$ are already $\mathbf m_F$. This proves the claim over $\mathbb R$, regardless of whether the moment values are rational. Equivalently, $P$ has rank $N-r$, its image equals $\ker W$, and its identity rules make it the identity on that kernel. ◻

</details>

Here is how `eliminate` verifies the hypotheses of the lemma. Its first modular elimination uses the prime $1000000007$. The routine `echelon` retains an original integer equation whenever that equation supplies a new pivot. These retained equations form $W$. Each pivot is at the largest remaining coordinate of its row; normalized elimination therefore proves that $W_J$ is invertible modulo the prime. Back substitution processes pivot coordinates in ascending order. When a smaller pivot is removed, its already reduced row contains only that pivot and free coordinates. The final rule for a pivot thus has no other pivot on its right side.

The program then works modulo the further moduli listed in the source, retaining matching pivot sets, combines coefficients by the Chinese remainder algorithm, and uses `rat` to propose rational rules. These steps preserve the absence of other pivot coordinates. All nonpivot coordinates receive explicit identity rules. Finally, for each retained integer equation the program substitutes every proposed rule, clears all denominators, and checks that every integer coefficient is zero. This is the exact check $WP=0$ in Lemma <a href="#lem:exact-elimination" data-reference-type="ref" data-reference="lem:exact-elimination">14</a>.

Every actual moment vector satisfies the selected equations, so this check suffices even if additional generated equations have not been used. In particular, the modular pivot count is not asserted to be the full rational rank of the original system. A prime that conceals an additional independent equation merely leaves that true equation unused. The rational reconstruction and its size threshold are ways to find a candidate $P$; the exact check and the lemma establish its validity.

<a id="pm-h-23"></a>
#### Pair equations and the vanishing identity

For an integer array $A$ with zero row and column sums, put $$\operatorname{wt}(A)=\sum_i\mathop{\mathrm{ord}}(A_{i,*}).$$ This also equals the sum of the column orders. Arrays name the real injective moments $m_H(A)$ of Section <a href="#sec:pair" data-reference-type="ref" data-reference="sec:pair">3</a>; the empty array names the constant moment $1$. A formal linear expression in these moments is stored as a finite list of rational coefficients indexed by arrays. Coefficients at the same index are added, and zero coefficients are discarded. These are the operations `add` and `lin` in the program.

The routine `canonpair` selects a representative using precisely the symmetries of $m_H$: permutation of either axis, removal of zero rows and columns, global negation, and transposition. More explicitly, after removing zero axes it considers $A,-A,A^{\mathsf T},-A^{\mathsf T}$, retains orientations with at least as many rows as columns, orders columns by their positive-part orders, and minimizes over the remaining column permutations after lexicographically sorting the rows. Each operation preserves the moment by its definition and Lemma <a href="#lem:contraction" data-reference-type="ref" data-reference="lem:contraction">4</a>. The degree orders and lexicographic choices serve only to select consistent names.

Define $U_0(A)$ recursively as a linear expression in representatives whose nonzero row and column orders are all at least two. If a row of $A$, or of $A^{\mathsf T}$, has order one, apply Lemma <a href="#lem:contraction" data-reference-type="ref" data-reference="lem:contraction">4</a> in that orientation: <a id="eq:recursive-pair-reduction"></a>$$U_0(A)=-\frac{1}{7-p}\sum_{\ell\ne i}U_0(A_{i\to\ell}),
 \qquad p\le6.
 \label{eq:recursive-pair-reduction}$$ Canonicalize each contracted array before continuing. If no such row exists on either axis, retain its moment as a single coordinate. The empty array is retained in the same way. Every contraction reduces the sum of the two axis lengths, so this recursion terminates. This is `evpair`; `expand_degree1` supplies the unsigned sum of contractions. The program checks that every evaluated array has both dimensions at most six and has zero sums on both axes.

The pair table is formed from formal seven-row equations. The exact enumeration restrictions are as follows:

1.  Choose a total weight $k\in\{7,8,9,10,11\}$ and a column count $q$ with $2\le q\le\min(4,\lfloor k/2\rfloor)$.

2.  Choose nondecreasing sequences of seven row orders $(r_0,\ldots,r_6)$ and $q$ column orders $(c_0,\ldots,c_{q-1})$. Both sequences sum to $k$, and $$1\le r_i\le\lfloor k/2\rfloor,
     \qquad 2\le c_j\le\lfloor k/2\rfloor.$$

3.  Enumerate integer $7\times q$ arrays with zero row and column sums and exactly these orders. Rows of equal order may be put in lexicographic order, and arrays with the same canonical representative may be skipped.

The routines `parts`, `comps`, and `allAB` implement this enumeration. A row is generated as the difference of disjointly supported nonnegative compositions of its prescribed order, within the remaining positive and negative column capacities. The capacities enforce the prescribed column orders. The first row has order one: seven positive integer row orders with sum at most eleven cannot all be at least two, and the orders are sorted.

For each retained array, the seven-row boundary case of Lemma <a href="#lem:contraction" data-reference-type="ref" data-reference="lem:contraction">4</a> gives the equation <a id="eq:pair-table-equations"></a>$$\sum_{\ell=1}^{6}U_0(A_{0\to\ell})=0.
 \label{eq:pair-table-equations}$$ Here $0,\ldots,6$ index the formal rows, not coordinates of a charge. Only the contracted arrays, which have at most six rows, are evaluated. The checks in `pairtable` verify the balance, number of formal rows, and order of the first row before forming this equation. Skipping an array can only omit an available true equation; the proof does not require a complete set of moment relations.

<a id="eq:verified-partition-expansion"></a>
Apply Lemma <a href="#lem:exact-elimination" data-reference-type="ref" data-reference="lem:exact-elimination">14</a> to these equations. Write $U(A)$ for the resulting expansion of $U_0(A)$ in the retained moment coordinates. For a product used here, with charge columns $a_1,\ldots,a_n$ and $n\le4$, let $A_{\mathcal S}$ be the array obtained by adding the columns in each block of a set partition $\mathcal S$ of $\{1,\ldots,n\}$. The final pair expansion is $$\sum_{\substack{\mathcal S\text{ a set partition}\\|\mathcal S|\le6}}
 \frac{(6)_{|\mathcal S|}}{6^n}\,U(A_{\mathcal S}).
 \label{eq:verified-partition-expansion}$$ This is exactly Lemma <a href="#lem:partitions" data-reference-type="ref" data-reference="lem:partitions">5</a>. The routine `partition` enumerates set partitions by numbering blocks in order of first occurrence, and `rep_pair` implements <a href="#eq:verified-partition-expansion" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="eq:verified-partition-expansion">[eq:verified-partition-expansion]</a>. The routine `apair` first forms the array of charge columns. It may remove a zero charge before the expansion, since that removes the factor $g_H(0)=1$. Deletion of zero coordinate rows is also legitimate: a uniform injection on the retained positions has the required uniform marginal.

For the pair certificate, `check_pair` forms the displayed polynomial $L_H$ of Proposition <a href="#prop:pair-certificate" data-reference-type="ref" data-reference="prop:pair-certificate">6</a>, with coefficients $-1,2,1,1,1$. It multiplies each monomial by the conjugate of every monomial, expanding $3|L_H|^2$ through <a href="#eq:verified-partition-expansion" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="eq:verified-partition-expansion">[eq:verified-partition-expansion]</a>. For each of the two stated charges $q$, it adds the expansion with charge columns $(\alpha,q,-\alpha,-q)$. After the exact pair substitutions, the result has no nonzero coefficient, for either $q$. This proves the identity in Proposition <a href="#prop:pair-certificate" data-reference-type="ref" data-reference="prop:pair-certificate">6</a>; all expressions used in the check have been identified with their actual averaged moment values above.

<a id="pm-h-24"></a>
#### Mixed keys and the finite mixed table

We use the notation $\mathcal M(\mathbf s;\mathbf a)$ and $m(A)$ from Section <a href="#sec:mixed" data-reference-type="ref" data-reference="sec:mixed">6</a>. The latter is the average of $m_H(A)$ over the ordered basis pairs. A mixed key consists of a label tuple $\mathbf s$ and an array $A$ whose columns are the charges. The array has at most six nonzero coordinate rows. Its rows and columns sum to zero. The routine `cmix` chooses representatives by simultaneous factor and label permutations, renaming labels by order of first occurrence, sorting the common coordinate rows, global negation, and removing zero factors and zero coordinate rows. These are exactly the symmetries justified in Lemma <a href="#lem:mixed-reductions" data-reference-type="ref" data-reference="lem:mixed-reductions">12</a>. The separate tag $(\varnothing,A)$ names the pair moment $m(A)$; the completely empty key names $1$.

The routine `evmix` supplies the following recursive expansion of a key into named moment coordinates:

1.  If a charge has order one or is a permutation of $\alpha$, the mixed moment is zero.

2.  When at most one label remains, expand the product by <a href="#eq:verified-partition-expansion" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="eq:verified-partition-expansion">[eq:verified-partition-expansion]</a>, now using the averaged pair moments $m(A)$, and attach the pair tag to its coordinates. If this single-label expression was zero by the preceding rule, also record its pair expansion as a zero equation. These recorded equations are stored in `rows0`.

3.  Otherwise, if an order-two charge has a label occurring exactly once among the factors, let $l$ be the number of labels in use and replace that label in turn by each of the other $l-1$ labels. The original moment is $-1/(7-l)$ times the sum of the resulting moments. Apply the same rules recursively to each term.

4.  If none of these rules applies, retain the key as an original mixed-moment coordinate.

The zero rules and the singleton coefficient follow from Lemma <a href="#lem:mixed-reductions" data-reference-type="ref" data-reference="lem:mixed-reductions">12</a>. Each singleton replacement decreases the number of distinct labels. The single-label use of the pair table is legitimate because the real average over ordered pairs includes the required transpose symmetry, as proved in that lemma.

The finite enumeration of triples is given by `triples(11)`. For precision, it generates balanced charges $a,b,c\in\mathbb Z^6$ with $$\begin{gathered}
 a+b+c=0,\qquad 0\le\mathop{\mathrm{ord}}(c)\le\mathop{\mathrm{ord}}(b)\le\mathop{\mathrm{ord}}(a)\le5,\\
 \mathop{\mathrm{ord}}(a)+\mathop{\mathrm{ord}}(b)+\mathop{\mathrm{ord}}(c)\le11.
 \end{gathered}$$ Choose $a$ up to a coordinate permutation and sign by taking the lesser of its sorted entries and the sorted entries of $-a$. Enumerate all balanced $b$ of orders at most $\mathop{\mathrm{ord}}(a)$, put $c=-a-b$, and retain those meeting the displayed restrictions. Sorting the six rows of the array $(a,b,c)$ removes common-coordinate duplicates. Balanced vectors of a given order are generated by subtracting two disjointly supported nonnegative compositions of that order, which is the routine `vecs`.

For each generated array, `mixeq` first evaluates all five equality patterns on its three labels, registering any single-label zero equations. It then considers the distinct permutations of the three charge columns, choosing $a\le b$ lexicographically to avoid duplicated first-two-column orderings. Write $$a_{ij}=a+e_i-e_j,\qquad b_{ij}=b-e_i+e_j
 \quad (0\le i,j\le5).$$ The following equations are included after applying `evmix` to every term:

- For different first two labels, sum the moments with charges $(a_{ij},b_{ij},c)$ over all $i\ne j$, obtaining zero by <a href="#eq:different-matrices" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="eq:different-matrices">[eq:different-matrices]</a>. For nonzero $c$, use the label tuples $(0,1,2)$, $(0,1,0)$, and $(0,1,1)$; for $c=0$, use $(0,1,0)$. Include this equation only if all $36$ shifted triples have total order at most eleven.

- For equal first two labels, fix $i$, sum over all six $j$, and subtract the moment with charges $(-c,c)$ and labels $(0,u)$, by <a href="#eq:same-matrix" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="eq:same-matrix">[eq:same-matrix]</a>. Use $(0,0,u)$ with $u=0,1$ when $c\ne0$, and $u=0$ when $c=0$. Also use the reversed shifts $(a+e_j-e_i,b-e_j+e_i,c)$. Include each equation only if all its six shifted triples have total order at most eleven.

The third charge is unchanged in these sums because the equations have been multiplied by its factor. On the right side of the equal-label equation, $a+b=-c$, which explains both remaining charges and the labels selected by `s[-2:]` in the source.

Finally add the pair equations recorded in `rows0`. All coordinates encountered by these reductions are registered, including coordinates that cancel in a particular equation. With the specified coordinate order, `mixtable` applies Lemma <a href="#lem:exact-elimination" data-reference-type="ref" data-reference="lem:exact-elimination">14</a> to the nonzero equations. A vanishing expression encountered later may still be replaced by zero: its vanishing is an independently proved rule, whether or not its pair expansion was used to strengthen this table. No completeness of the enumeration, and no vanishing of unlisted higher-order moments, is assumed.

The exact table dimensions are as follows. The witness column counts the independent equations used in Lemma <a href="#lem:exact-elimination" data-reference-type="ref" data-reference="lem:exact-elimination">14</a>.

<div class="center">

| Table | Nonzero equations | Coordinates | Witness rows |
|:------|------------------:|------------:|-------------:|
| Pair  |            $8871$ |       $909$ |        $897$ |
| Mixed |           $46041$ |      $3429$ |       $2767$ |

</div>

<a id="pm-h-25"></a>
#### Positive matrices and the negative bound

For the mixed certificate, the literal data in Appendix <a href="#app:code" data-reference-type="ref" data-reference="app:code">8</a> specify the six lists and lower triangles of the symmetric integer matrices $Z_\lambda$. The routine `makeZ` checks the number of entries and reconstructs each symmetric matrix. Positive definiteness is verified without eigenvalue approximation. At each step, `posdef` writes the remaining matrix as $$B=\begin{pmatrix}C&u\\u^{\mathsf T}&p\end{pmatrix},
 \qquad p>40000,$$ and replaces it by the exact Schur complement $C-uu^{\mathsf T}/p$. The identity $$\begin{pmatrix}x\\t\end{pmatrix}^{\!*}
 B\begin{pmatrix}x\\t\end{pmatrix}
 =x^*(C-uu^{\mathsf T}/p)x
   +p\left|t+\frac{u^{\mathsf T}x}{p}\right|^2$$ shows inductively that positive pivots prove positive definiteness, also for complex vectors. All $98$ pivots in the six matrices satisfy the asserted rational inequality.

The routines `permsign` and `polynomial` implement the signed permutation sums defining $Y_\lambda$ in Section <a href="#sec:mixed" data-reference-type="ref" data-reference="sec:mixed">6</a>. The convention $(P_pv)_i=v_{p(i)}$ gives $(P_pP_qv)_i=v_{q(p(i))}$, exactly `perm(perm(v,q),p)`. The routine `tagmom` uses the Gram charges $(a,-v,v-a)$ of Lemma <a href="#lem:gram" data-reference-type="ref" data-reference="lem:gram">11</a>. After multiplying the entire form by five, `product` assigns weight five unless both function types are one; in that case the weights for equal and different external labels are one and four. The lower-triangle summation in `gaps` doubles the off-diagonal terms. Since the polynomial coefficients and $Z_\lambda$ are real, its real-moment expansion equals the full quadratic form for complex $Y_\lambda$.

<a id="eq:exact-residual-coefficients"></a>
Consequently the expression formed by `gaps` is exactly $$\mathcal Q=
 5\sum_\lambda 10^{15-e_\lambda}
       \mathbb E\bigl(Y_\lambda^*Z_\lambda Y_\lambda\bigr).$$ Apply `evmix` and then the certified mixed substitutions. On combining coefficients, we obtain the residual expansion in <a href="#eq:mixed-residual" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="eq:mixed-residual">[eq:mixed-residual]</a>, now indexed by the program’s moment keys: $$\begin{gathered}
 \mathcal Q=c_0+\sum_{K\ne\varnothing}c_K\,\mu_K,\\
 c_0=-\frac{613302797399911}{6480},\qquad
 \sum_{K\ne\varnothing}|c_K|
     =\frac{179721388988719}{6480}.
 \end{gathered}
 \label{eq:exact-residual-coefficients}$$ Here $\mu_K$ denotes the original moment named by a retained coordinate; the notation $K\ne\varnothing$ excludes the completely empty constant key. Both equalities of rational coefficients in <a href="#eq:exact-residual-coefficients" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="eq:exact-residual-coefficients">[eq:exact-residual-coefficients]</a> are checked exactly by the program, as is their strictly negative sum.

It remains to justify the bound on these residual coordinates. A tagged pair coordinate is a real average of unit-modulus monomials, so $|m(A)|\le1$. A mixed coordinate is a real average of products of $g_s(a)$, each of absolute value at most one, so $|\mathcal M(\mathbf s;\mathbf a)|\le1$. Every coordinate retained by the substitutions is one of these original moments. The new linear combinations occur as expressions in the coordinates, not as newly named coordinates to which a bound is assigned. The constant coordinate is exactly one. Hence $$0\le\mathcal Q
 \le c_0+\sum_{K\ne\varnothing}|c_K|
 =-\frac{2007321335237}{30}<0.$$ This proves Proposition <a href="#prop:mixed-certificate" data-reference-type="ref" data-reference="prop:mixed-certificate">13</a>. Together with the pair coefficient check, it completes the exact verification of both certificates using only the finite equations and integer data specified in Appendix <a href="#app:code" data-reference-type="ref" data-reference="app:code">8</a>.

<a id="app:code"></a>
### Complete exact verifier

This appendix gives the exact verifier for Propositions <a href="#prop:pair-certificate" data-reference-type="ref" data-reference="prop:pair-certificate">6</a> and <a href="#prop:mixed-certificate" data-reference-type="ref" data-reference="prop:mixed-certificate">13</a>. The cubic character counts are specified by the finite sums and loops following Table <a href="#tab:cubic-counts" data-reference-type="ref" data-reference="tab:cubic-counts">1</a>.

The program below uses only the Python standard library and runs with Python 3.8 or later, with assertions enabled. Its standalone file is `verification/verify.py`. The accompanying wrapper `verification/run.py` checks that assertions are enabled and that the verifier has the expected checksum before running it. From the paper directory, execute

<div class="center">

`python3 verification/run.py`

</div>

The complete expected output is

```
rank table: 8871 909 897
rank table: 46041 3429 2767
-613302797399911/6480 179721388988719/6480 5000000000000000
```

In each `rank table` line, the three entries give the number of nonzero equations, the number of moment coordinates, and the number of independent witness rows used for substitution, respectively. The final line gives the constant coefficient, the sum of the absolute values of all nonconstant coefficients, and the overall normalizing scale $5\cdot10^{15}$. Dividing the first two entries by this scale gives the constant and residual coefficient norm for $\sum_\lambda 10^{-e_\lambda}\mathbb E(Y_\lambda^*Z_\lambda Y_\lambda)$. Every assertion in the program is checked in integer or rational arithmetic.

For reference, the SHA-256 checksum of `verification/verify.py` is

<div class="center">

104a7306205b8544c8f913f6afdb59603f2e 04a207c9ac592c383b80eb0e2aa9

</div>

The literal `data` near the end specifies each shape, its ordered list of function types and charges, its decimal scale exponent, and the lower triangle of its symmetric integer matrix, in row order.

Equation numbers in the code comments use the original numbering: the reference in `expand_degree1` is to the contractions of Lemma <a href="#lem:contraction" data-reference-type="ref" data-reference="lem:contraction">4</a>, and those in `mixeq` are to <a href="#eq:same-matrix" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="eq:same-matrix">[eq:same-matrix]</a> and <a href="#eq:different-matrices" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="eq:different-matrices">[eq:different-matrices]</a>.

``` python
```

<a id="pm-h-27"></a>
#### References

<div id="refs" class="references csl-bib-body hanging-indent">

</div>
